\mathcal{F}
constructed in Remark
5.46
.
Notice that for all
\bar{a}\in\bar{\mathcal{A}}
,
\Lambda_{0,\bar{a}}
and
\Lambda_{\bar{a}}

have the same rank, since by hypothesis
the Sen weights of
\Lambda_{0}
and
\Lambda
are the same and have the same multiplicity
modulo
\mathbb{Z}
.
Let then
\mu_{\bar{a}}\in X_{*}(T)^{+}
such that
\Lambda_{\bar{a}}\in\mathrm{Gr}_{n_{\bar{a}}}^{\mu_{\bar{a}},\mathrm{rig}}
;
in other words, there is a basis
\{b_{\bar{a},1},\dots,b_{\bar{a},n_{\bar{a}}}\}
of

\Lambda_{0,\bar{a}}
such that
\Lambda_{\bar{a}}
is spanned by

\{t^{\mu_{\bar{a},1}}b_{\bar{a},1},\dots,t^{\mu_{\bar{a},n_{\bar{a}}}}b_{\bar{a},n_{\bar{a}}}\}
.
Moreover let
\{y_{\bar{a},1},\dots,y_{\bar{a},n_{\bar{a}}}\}
be the basis of
\Lambda_{0,\bar{a}}

constructed in Remark
5.46
.
Since
\Lambda_{\bar{a}}
is
\Gamma_{m}
-stable, we can choose the basis
\{b_{\bar{a},1},\dots,b_{\bar{a},n_{\bar{a}}}\}

such that
b_{\bar{a},i}=\sum_{j=1}^{n_{\bar{a}}}a_{j}y_{\bar{a},j}
with
a_{j}\in A

by Lemma
5.49
.
Furthermore by definition of
\pi_{\mu}
(
5.7
), we have

(5.19)

\pi_{\mu_{\bar{a}}}(\Lambda_{\bar{a}})=(t^{-\mu_{\bar{a},\bullet}}\Lambda_{\bar{a}}\cap\Lambda_{0,\bar{a}})/(t^{-\mu_{\bar{a},\bullet}}\Lambda_{\bar{a}}\cap t\Lambda_{0,\bar{a}})=\bigoplus_{\mu_{\bar{a},j}\leqslant\mu_{\bar{a},\bullet}}A\cdot b_{\bar{a},j}.

Observe that

\displaystyle\mathrm{Fil}^{i}_{W_{\bar{a}}^{+}}(D_{\mathrm{pdR},\bar{a}})(W_{\bar{a}})

\displaystyle=\left(t^{i}B_{\mathrm{dR}}^{+}[\log(t)]\otimes_{B_{\mathrm{dR}}^{+}}(\Lambda_{\bar{a}}\otimes_{K_{m}\llbracket t\rrbracket}B_{\mathrm{dR}}^{+})\otimes_{B_{\mathrm{dR}}^{+}}W_{\mathrm{dR}}^{+}(\delta_{\bar{a}}^{-1})\right)^{G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}

\displaystyle=\left(B_{\mathrm{dR}}^{+}[\log(t)]\otimes_{B_{\mathrm{dR}}^{+}}(t^{i}\Lambda_{\bar{a}}\otimes_{K_{m}\llbracket t\rrbracket}B_{\mathrm{dR}}^{+})\otimes_{B_{\mathrm{dR}}^{+}}W_{\mathrm{dR}}^{+}(\delta_{\bar{a}}^{-1})\right)^{G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}.

Notice that
(\Lambda_{\bar{a}}\otimes_{K_{m}\llbracket t\rrbracket}B_{\mathrm{dR}}^{+})\otimes_{B_{\mathrm{dR}}^{+}}W_{\mathrm{dR}}^{+}(\delta_{\bar{a}}^{-1})
is a
G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
-stable
B_{\mathrm{dR},A}^{+}
-lattice with integral Sen weights,
thanks to the twist by
W_{\mathrm{dR}}^{+}(\delta_{\bar{a}})
.
For
j
such that
\mu_{\bar{a},j}\leqslant-i
, we have that

b_{\bar{a},j}=\sum_{k=1}^{n_{\bar{a}}}a_{k}y_{\bar{a},k}\in t^{i}\Lambda_{\bar{a}}\otimes_{K_{m}\llbracket t\rrbracket}B_{\mathrm{dR}}^{+}
.
We will show that for all
k\geqslant 0
and for all
j
such that
\mu_{\bar{a},j}\leqslant-i
we have

\nabla^{k}(b_{\bar{a},j})\in t^{i}\Lambda_{\bar{a}}\otimes_{K_{m}\llbracket t\rrbracket}B_{\mathrm{dR}}^{+}:

it is sufficient to prove that
\gamma^{k}(b_{\bar{a},j})\in t^{i}\Lambda_{\bar{a}}\otimes_{K_{m}\llbracket t\rrbracket}B_{\mathrm{dR}}^{+}

for all
k\geqslant 0
;
this is true, since the filtration
\pi_{\mu_{\bar{a}}}(\Lambda_{\bar{a}})
is

\Gamma_{m}
-stable by Lemma
5.49
, hence

\gamma^{k}(b_{\bar{a},j})\in\bigoplus_{\mu_{\bar{a},l}\leqslant-i}A\llbracket t\rrbracket\cdot b_{\bar{a},l}
.
As a consequence, if we let
b_{\bar{a},j}^{\prime}
denote the element corresponding to
b_{\bar{a},j}

through the isomorphism of
B_{\mathrm{dR},A}^{+}
-modules

(t^{i}\Lambda_{\bar{a}}\otimes_{K_{m}\llbracket t\rrbracket}B_{\mathrm{dR}}^{+})\otimes_{B_{\mathrm{dR}}^{+}}W_{\mathrm{dR}}^{+}(\delta_{\bar{a}}^{-1})\rightarrow t^{i}\Lambda_{\bar{a}}\otimes_{K_{m}\llbracket t\rrbracket}B_{\mathrm{dR}}^{+},

we have that
\nabla^{k}(b^{\prime}_{\bar{a},j})\in(t^{i}\Lambda_{\bar{a}}\otimes_{K_{m}\llbracket t\rrbracket}B_{\mathrm{dR}}^{+})\otimes_{B_{\mathrm{dR}}^{+}}W_{\mathrm{dR}}^{+}(\delta_{\bar{a}}^{-1})
for all
k\geqslant 0
.
Observe that
\nabla^{k}(b^{\prime}_{\bar{a},j})=\sum_{i=1}^{n_{\bar{a}}}a_{i}\nabla^{k}(y^{\prime}_{\bar{a},i})
, so
by construction of
\{y_{\bar{a},1}^{\prime},\dots,y^{\prime}_{\bar{a},n_{\bar{a}}}\}
in Remark
5.46
,
we have
\nabla^{K+1}(b^{\prime}_{\bar{a},j})=0
for some
K\geqslant 0
.
Thus

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(b_{\bar{a},j})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{K}\frac{(-1)^{k}}{k!}\nabla^{k}(b^{\prime}_{\bar{a},j})(\log(t))^{k}=\sum_{i=1}^{n_{\bar{a}}}a_{i}\left(\sum_{k=0}^{K}\frac{(-1)^{k}}{k!}\nabla^{k}(y^{\prime}_{\bar{a},j})(\log(t))^{k}\right)

\displaystyle\in B_{\mathrm{dR}}^{+}[\log(t)]\otimes_{B_{\mathrm{dR}}^{+}}(t^{i}\Lambda_{\bar{a}}\otimes_{K_{m}\llbracket t\rrbracket}B_{\mathrm{dR}}^{+})\otimes_{B_{\mathrm{dR}}^{+}}W_{\mathrm{dR}}^{+}(\delta_{\bar{a}}^{-1}).

Now notice that
\sum_{k=0}^{K}\frac{(-1)^{k}}{k!}\nabla^{k}(y^{\prime}_{\bar{a},j})(\log(t))^{k}\in D_{\mathrm{pdR},\bar{a}}(W_{\bar{a}})

by the construction in Remark
5.46
, thus

\mathcal{F}(b_{\bar{a},j})=\sum_{k=0}^{K}\frac{(-1)^{k}}{k!}\nabla^{k}(b^{\prime}_{\bar{a},j})(\log(t))^{k}\in\mathrm{Fil}^{i}_{W_{\bar{a}}^{+}}(D_{\mathrm{pdR},\bar{a}}(W_{\bar{a}}))

and they constitute an
A
-basis of
\mathrm{Fil}^{i}_{W_{\bar{a}}^{+}}(D_{\mathrm{pdR},\bar{a}}(W_{\bar{a}}))
.
Finally, observe that

\displaystyle\pi_{\mu_{\bar{a}}}(\Lambda_{\bar{a}})=\bigoplus_{\mu_{\bar{a},j}\leqslant\mu_{\bar{a},\bullet}}A\cdot b_{\bar{a},j}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{F}}

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{\mu_{\bar{a},j}\leqslant\mu_{\bar{a},\bullet}}A\cdot\mathcal{F}(b_{\bar{a},j})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Fil}_{W_{\bar{a}}^{+}}^{-\mu_{\bar{a},\bullet}}(D_{\mathrm{pdR},\bar{a}}(W_{\bar{a}})).

∎

6.
Trianguline representations of dimension 2

In this section we prove that any trianguline representation of dimension 2 is in the
trianguline variety.
More precisely, we show the following Theorem, which is a special case of Theorem
B
.

Theorem 6.1
.

Let
\rho:G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\rightarrow GL_{2}(\mathcal{O}_{L})
be a trianguline representation
and
\overline{\rho}:G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\rightarrow GL_{2}(k_{L})
a reduction of
\rho

to the residue field of
L
.
Let
(\delta_{1},\delta_{2})\in\mathcal{T}^{2}(L)
be the parameters of
\rho
.
We have
(\rho,(\delta_{1},\delta_{2}))\in X_{\mathrm{tri}}^{\square}(\overline{\rho})(L)
.

Theorem
6.1
is obviously true if
\delta_{1}/\delta_{2}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathrm{reg}}(L)
, because then

(\delta_{1},\delta_{2})\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathrm{reg}}_{2}(L)
.
In section 4 we proved that
(\rho,(\delta_{1},\delta_{2}))\in X_{\mathrm{tri}}^{\square}(\overline{\rho})(L)
if
\delta_{1}/\delta_{2}\in\mathcal{T}^{-}(L)
.
Therefore the only case left to treat is
\delta_{1}/\delta_{2}\in\mathcal{T}^{+}(L)
, which
is the most complicated: since
H^{2}_{\phi,\Gamma}(\mathcal{R}_{L}(\delta_{1}/\delta_{2}))\neq 0
,
it is not possible to choose a neighbourhood
\mathcal{U}
of
(\delta_{1},\delta_{2})

in
\mathcal{T}^{2}
such that
H^{2}_{\phi,\Gamma}(\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{U}}(\widetilde{\delta}_{1}/\widetilde{\delta}_{2}))

is locally free, where
(\widetilde{\delta}_{1},\widetilde{\delta}_{2})
are the characters
corresponding to
\mathcal{U}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{T}^{2}
.
This implies that we cannot use Theorem
4.2
to construct a rigid space with a
family of
(\phi,\Gamma)
-modules specializing to
\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{rig}}^{{\dagger}}(\rho)
, as we did
in the proof of Proposition
4.4
.
Of course this makes things more difficult, but we’ll see that it’s still possible
to somehow reduce ourselves to the case in which
\delta_{1}/\delta_{2}\notin\mathcal{T}^{+}(L)

with the machinery developed in section 5 and be able then to use the results of section 4 to construct such a
rigid space
X
with a family of
(\phi,\Gamma)
-modules
\mathbb{D}
over
X
.
The downside is now that the space
X
is no more a vector bundle over a neighbourhood

\mathcal{U}
of
(\delta_{1},\delta_{2})
.
Hence it will be harder to show that the family of
(\phi,\Gamma)
-modules
\mathbb{D}

is densely regular.

Let us assume then that
\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{rig}}^{{\dagger}}(\rho)
is an extension of
\mathcal{R}_{L}(\delta_{2})
by
\mathcal{R}_{L}(\delta_{1})

such that
\delta_{1}/\delta_{2}\in\mathcal{T}^{+}(L)
, which means that
\delta_{1}/\delta_{2}

is of the form
\chi x^{n}
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

6.1.
Construction of space of extensions

Lemma 6.2
.

Let
\delta_{1},\delta_{2}\in\mathcal{T}(L)
such that
\delta_{1}/\delta_{2}=\chi x^{n}
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
We have the isomorphism

(6.1)

H^{1}_{\phi,\Gamma}(\mathcal{R}_{L}(\delta_{1}/\delta_{2})[1/t])\cong H^{1}_{\phi,\Gamma}(\mathcal{R}_{L}(\chi x^{-1}))

of
L
-modules.

Proof.

By Remark
5.15
, we have an isomorphism

H^{1}_{\phi,\Gamma}(\mathcal{R}_{L}(\delta_{1}/\delta_{2})[1/t])\cong\lim_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rightarrow}}{{i}}}H^{1}_{\phi,\Gamma}(\mathcal{R}_{L}(\delta_{1}/\delta_{2}x^{-i}))=\lim_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rightarrow}}{{i}}}H^{1}_{\phi,\Gamma}(\mathcal{R}_{L}(\chi x^{n-i})).

Let us consider the character
\chi x^{-1}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathrm{wr}}(L)\cap\mathcal{T}^{\mathrm{reg}}(L)
,
which has weight
0
.
By Lemma
5.16
, we have that

H^{1}_{\phi,\Gamma}(\mathcal{R}_{L}(\chi x^{-1}))\rightarrow H^{1}_{\phi,\Gamma}(\mathcal{R}_{L}(\chi x^{-i}))

is an isomorphism for all
i\geqslant 1
.
This means that all the transition maps

H^{1}_{\phi,\Gamma}(\mathcal{R}_{L}(\chi x^{n-i}))\rightarrow H^{1}_{\phi,\Gamma}(\mathcal{R}_{L}(\chi x^{n-i-1}))

of the direct limit

\lim_{i}H^{1}_{\phi,\Gamma}(\mathcal{R}_{L}(\chi x^{n-i}))

are isomorphisms for
i\geqslant n+1
.
As a consequence, we have that

H^{1}_{\phi,\Gamma}(\mathcal{R}_{L}(\delta_{1}/\delta_{2})[1/t])\cong H^{1}_{\phi,\Gamma}(\mathcal{R}_{L}(\chi x^{-1})).

∎

Now let
\rho:G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\rightarrow GL_{2}(L)
be a trianguline representation whose
(\phi,\Gamma)
-module

\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{rig}}^{{\dagger}}(\rho)
is an extension of
\mathcal{R}_{L}(\delta_{2})
by
\mathcal{R}_{L}(\delta_{1})
.
Let us denote by
D^{\prime}\in H^{1}_{\phi,\Gamma}(\mathcal{R}_{L}(\chi x^{-1}))
the extension corresponding to

\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{rig}}^{{\dagger}}(\rho)[1/t]
through the isomorphism (
6.1
).
Notice that in particular we have that
\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{rig}}^{{\dagger}}(\rho)[1/t]=D^{\prime}[1/t]
.
Let
a_{1}\coloneqq\mathrm{wt}(\delta_{1})
and
a_{2}\coloneqq\mathrm{wt}(\delta_{2})
;
since
\delta_{1}/\delta_{2}=\chi x^{n}
, we have
a_{1}-a_{2}=n+1
.
If
a_{1},a_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}
, let
\mu_{1}=a_{1},\mu_{2}=a_{2}
; otherwise, let
\mu_{1}=0
and
\mu_{2}=-n-1
.

Remark 6.3
.

Observe that the characters
\delta_{1}x^{-\mu_{1}},\delta_{2}x^{-\mu_{2}}
either both have weights equal to 0
or they have the same non-integer weight.

The
(\phi,\Gamma)
-module
D^{\prime}
is an extension of
\mathcal{R}_{L}(\delta_{2}x^{-\mu_{2}})
by
\mathcal{R}_{L}(\delta_{1}x^{-\mu_{1}})
.
In fact we have that
-\mu_{1}+\mu_{2}=-\mathrm{wt}(\delta_{1})+\mathrm{wt}(\delta_{2})=-\mathrm{wt}(\delta_{1}/\delta_{2})=-n-1
,
thus
\delta_{1}x^{-\mu_{1}}/\delta_{2}x^{-\mu_{2}}=\delta_{1}/\delta_{2}\cdot x^{-n-1}=\chi x^{-1}
.
Since
\delta_{1}/\delta_{2}\cdot x^{-n-1}=\chi x^{-1}\notin\mathcal{T}^{+}(L)
by construction,
we can apply Proposition
4.4
and get the following result.

Proposition 6.4
.

There exist

•

a rigid space
X^{\prime}
over
L
which is also a vector bundle of dimension 1 over an
appropriate neighbourhood
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
of
(\delta_{1}x^{-\mu_{1}},\delta_{2}x^{-\mu_{2}})
in
\mathcal{T}^{\mathrm{reg}}_{2}
;

•

a family of
(\phi,\Gamma)
-modules
\mathbb{D}^{\prime}
over
X^{\prime}
;

•

a map
(\widetilde{\delta}_{1}^{\prime},\widetilde{\delta}_{2}^{\prime}):X^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathcal{T}^{2}

such that there are

(1)

x^{\prime}\in X^{\prime}
such that
\mathbb{D}^{\prime}\widehat{\otimes}k(x^{\prime})=D^{\prime}
and

(\widetilde{\delta}_{1}^{\prime},\widetilde{\delta}_{2}^{\prime})\widehat{\otimes}k(x^{\prime})=(\delta_{1}x^{-\mu_{1}},\delta_{2}x^{-\mu_{2}})
;

(2)

a Zariski open dense subset
U^{\prime}\xhookrightarrow{j}X^{\prime}
such that
j^{*}\mathbb{D}^{\prime}

has a filtration of sub-
(\phi,\Gamma)
-modules with graded pieces
\mathcal{R}_{U^{\prime}}(\widetilde{\delta}_{1}^{\prime})

and
\mathcal{R}_{U^{\prime}}(\widetilde{\delta}_{2}^{\prime})
and
j^{*}(\widetilde{\delta}_{1}^{\prime},\widetilde{\delta}_{2}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathrm{reg}}_{2}(U^{\prime}).

Remark 6.5
.

After eventually shrinking
X^{\prime}
to a neighbourhood of
x^{\prime}
, we may assume that
X^{\prime}=\mathrm{Sp}(R^{\prime})

is affinoid.
Notice moreover that the rigid space
X^{\prime}
and the family of extensions
\mathbb{D}^{\prime}

parametrizes all the extensions
H^{1}_{\phi,\Gamma}(\mathcal{R}_{X^{\prime}}(\widetilde{\delta}_{1}^{\prime}/\widetilde{\delta}_{2}^{\prime}))
. In other words, the map

\displaystyle\Psi_{Y}:X^{\prime}(Y)

\displaystyle\twoheadrightarrow H^{1}_{\phi,\Gamma}(\mathcal{R}_{Y}(g^{*}\widetilde{\delta}_{1}^{\prime}/g^{*}\widetilde{\delta}_{2}^{\prime}))

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle\mapsto f^{*}\mathbb{D}^{\prime}

from Theorem
4.2
is actually a bijection for all
g:Y\rightarrow\mathcal{U}^{\prime}

map of rigid spaces.
This follows from Theorem
4.2
: in fact
(\delta_{1}x^{-\mu_{1}},\delta_{2}x^{-\mu_{2}})\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathrm{reg}}_{2}(L)

and
\dim_{L}H^{1}_{\phi,\Gamma}(\mathcal{R}_{L}(\delta_{1}/\delta_{2}\cdot x^{-n-1}))=1
.
Therefore we can choose the neighbourhood
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
inside
\mathcal{T}^{\mathrm{reg}}_{2}
in such a way that

H^{1}_{\phi,\Gamma}(\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{U}^{\prime}}(\widetilde{\delta}_{1}^{\prime}/\widetilde{\delta}_{2}^{\prime}))
is free of rank 1
over
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{U}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{U}^{\prime})
.

By Theorem
5.6
, there exist
r_{0}\in p^{\mathbb{Q}}\cap[0,1)
and
a unique
(\phi,\Gamma)
-module
D^{r_{0}}
over
\mathcal{R}_{L}^{r_{0}}
such that
\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{rig}}^{{\dagger}}(\rho)=D^{r_{0}}\otimes_{\mathcal{R}^{r_{0}}_{L}}\mathcal{R}_{L}
;
moreover there is
r_{1}\in p^{\mathbb{Q}}\cap[0,1)
such that
the family of
(\phi,\Gamma)
-modules
\mathbb{D}^{\prime}
can be already defined over
\mathcal{R}_{X^{\prime}}^{r_{1}}
.
Let us denote by
r
the maximum of
r_{0}
and
r_{1}
, in such a way that both
\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{rig}}^{{\dagger}}(\rho)

and
\mathbb{D}^{\prime}
are defined over
\mathcal{R}_{L}^{r}
and
\mathcal{R}_{X^{\prime}}^{r}
respectively.
In other words, there exist
D^{r}
a
(\phi,\Gamma)
-module over
\mathcal{R}^{r}_{L}
such that

\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{rig}}^{{\dagger}}(\rho)=D^{r}\otimes_{\mathcal{R}^{r}_{L}}\mathcal{R}_{L}
and a
(\phi,\Gamma)
-module
\mathbb{D}^{\prime r}

over
\mathcal{R}_{X^{\prime}}^{r}

such that
\mathbb{D}^{\prime r}\otimes_{\mathcal{R}_{X^{\prime}}^{r}}\mathcal{R}_{X^{\prime}}=\mathbb{D}^{\prime}
.
Let
m(r)
be the smallest positive integer such that
p^{m-1}(p-1)\geqslant r
for all
m\geqslant m(r)
,
so that all the points of the form
1-\zeta_{p^{m}}
(where
\zeta_{p^{m}}
is a
p^{m}
-th root of unity)
lie in the open annulus
\mathbb{B}^{r}
.
Let us fix
m\geqslant m(r)
and let us assume that
L
contains
K_{m}

(by Lemma
3.4
we know we can always make this assumption).
As a consequence of Theorem
5.11
, the
(\phi,\Gamma)
-module
D^{r}
corresponds to the
tuple
(D^{r}[1/t],D^{r}\otimes_{\mathcal{R}_{L}^{r}}\prod_{K_{m}\hookrightarrow L}L\llbracket t\rrbracket,f)
through
the equivalence
5.2
, where

f:D^{r}[1/t]\otimes_{\mathcal{R}^{r}_{L}[1/t]}\prod_{K_{m}\hookrightarrow L}L((t))\xrightarrow{\sim}(D^{r}\otimes_{\mathcal{R}_{L}^{r}}\prod_{K_{m}\hookrightarrow L}L\llbracket t\rrbracket)[1/t]

is defined as in Theorem
5.11
.
Let us denote by
\Lambda
the
\Gamma
-stable finite projective module

D^{r}\otimes_{\mathcal{R}_{L}^{r}}\prod_{K_{m}\hookrightarrow L}L\llbracket t\rrbracket
.
By Remark
5.12
, we have

\Lambda=\prod_{K_{m}\xhookrightarrow{\tau}L}\Lambda_{\tau}

where
\Lambda_{\tau}
is a
\Gamma_{m}
-stable lattice over
L\llbracket t\rrbracket
.
On the other hand, given such a lattice
\Lambda_{\tau}
, it is possible to recover
\Lambda
.
From now on, since the two notions are equivalent,
we fix an embedding
\tau:K_{m}\hookrightarrow L
and we will denote
by
\Lambda
the
\Gamma_{m}
-stable lattice
\Lambda_{\tau}
over
L\llbracket t\rrbracket
.
In the same way, let
\Lambda^{\prime}
be the
\Gamma_{m}
-stable lattice over

L\llbracket t\rrbracket
corresponding to the
\tau
-factor of the lattice associated to
D^{\prime}
.
Let us denote by
\mathbb{L}^{\prime}
the
\Gamma_{m}
-stable lattice corresponding to the
factor
\tau:K_{m}\hookrightarrow L
of the lattice
\mathbb{D}^{\prime r}\otimes_{\mathcal{R}^{r}_{X^{\prime}}}\prod_{K_{m}\hookrightarrow L}R^{\prime}\llbracket t\rrbracket
associated to
\mathbb{D}^{\prime}
.
After choosing a basis of
\mathbb{D}^{\prime}
,
we choose a trivialization of
\mathbb{L}^{\prime}
and fix a basis of
\mathbb{L}^{\prime}

respecting the triangulation of
\mathbb{D}^{\prime}
.

We then have the following result.

Lemma 6.6
.

\Lambda\in Y_{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}(L)
.

Proof.

We have to show that
\mathrm{gr}^{1}(\Lambda)=t^{\mu_{1}}\mathrm{gr}^{1}(\Lambda^{\prime})
and

\mathrm{gr}^{2}(\Lambda)=t^{\mu_{2}}\cdot\mathrm{gr}^{2}(\Lambda^{\prime})
.
Let us denote by
\delta_{1}^{\prime}\coloneqq\delta_{1}x^{-\mu_{1}}
and
\delta_{2}^{\prime}\coloneqq\delta_{2}x^{-\mu_{2}}
.
Since we chose a basis of
\Lambda^{\prime}
respecting the triangulation of
D^{\prime}
, we have that

\mathrm{Fil}^{\bullet}(L((t))^{2})

is induced by the filtration of
D^{\prime}[1/t]
.
This implies that

\mathrm{gr}^{1}(\Lambda^{\prime})=\mathcal{R}_{L}^{r}(\delta_{1}^{\prime})\otimes_{\mathcal{R}_{L}^{r},\tau}L\llbracket t\rrbracket

and

\mathrm{gr}^{2}(\Lambda^{\prime})=\mathcal{R}_{L}^{r}(\delta_{2}^{\prime})\otimes_{\mathcal{R}_{L}^{r},\tau}L\llbracket t\rrbracket.

Let us denote by
V
the module
L((t))^{2}
for simplicity.
We have

\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}^{1}(\Lambda)

\displaystyle=\Lambda\cap\mathrm{Fil}^{1}(V)=\Lambda\cap\left(\mathcal{R}_{L}^{r}(\delta_{1}^{\prime})[1/t]\otimes_{\mathcal{R}^{r}_{L}[1/t],\tau}L((t))\right)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{R}_{L}^{r}(\delta_{1})\otimes_{\mathcal{R}_{L}^{r},\tau}L\llbracket t\rrbracket=t^{\mu_{1}}\mathcal{R}_{L}^{r}(\delta_{1}x^{-\mu_{1}})\otimes_{\mathcal{R}_{L}^{r},\tau}L\llbracket t\rrbracket=t^{\mu_{1}}\cdot\mathrm{gr}^{1}(\Lambda^{\prime})

and

\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}^{2}(\Lambda)

\displaystyle=\Lambda/\mathrm{gr}^{1}(\Lambda)=\left(D^{r}\otimes_{\mathcal{R}_{L}^{r},\tau}L\llbracket t\rrbracket\right)/\left(\mathcal{R}_{L}^{r}(\delta_{1})\otimes_{\mathcal{R}_{L}^{r},\tau}L\llbracket t\rrbracket\right)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{R}_{L}^{r}(\delta_{2})\otimes_{\mathcal{R}_{L}^{r},\tau}L\llbracket t\rrbracket=\mathcal{R}_{L}^{r}(\delta_{2}^{\prime}\cdot x^{\mu_{2}})\otimes_{\mathcal{R}_{L}^{r},\tau}L\llbracket t\rrbracket

\displaystyle=t^{\mu_{2}}\cdot\mathcal{R}^{r}_{L}(\delta_{2}^{\prime})\otimes_{\mathcal{R}_{L}^{r},\tau}L\llbracket t\rrbracket=t^{\mu_{2}}\cdot\mathrm{gr}^{2}(\Lambda^{\prime}).

∎

Consider the space
X^{\prime}\times\mathrm{Gr}_{2}^{\mathrm{rig}}
, where
X^{\prime}
is the rigid
space defined in Proposition
6.4
;
we will define a subspace consisting of
\Gamma_{m}
-stable lattices.
First of all, let us fix a topological generator
\gamma
of the group
\Gamma_{m}
.

Definition 6.7
.

Let us define the map
\gamma\cdot:X^{\prime}\times\mathrm{Gr}_{2}^{\mathrm{rig}}\rightarrow\mathrm{Gr}_{2}^{\mathrm{rig}}
in the following way:
for any affinoid
L
-algebra
A
and for any
(y^{\prime},\Lambda_{A})\in(X^{\prime}\times\mathrm{Gr}_{2}^{\mathrm{rig}})(A)
, let

\gamma\cdot(y^{\prime},\Lambda_{A})\coloneqq\gamma\cdot\Lambda_{A},

where the action of
\gamma
on
\Lambda_{A}
is induced from the
action of
\Gamma_{m}
on
y^{\prime*}\mathbb{D}^{\prime}
through the isomorphism

(y^{\prime*}\mathbb{D}^{\prime r})[1/t]\otimes_{\mathcal{R}_{A}^{r}[1/t],\tau}A((t))\cong\Lambda_{A}[1/t].

Notice that
\gamma\cdot\Lambda_{A}\in\mathrm{Gr}^{\mathrm{rig}}_{2}(A)
, because
\gamma
induces an automorphism on

(y^{\prime*}\mathbb{D}^{\prime r})[1/t]
, hence the map is well-defined.

Theorem 6.8
.

There exist

•

an affinoid rigid space
X=\mathrm{Sp}(R)
over
L
;

•

a family of triangulable
(\phi,\Gamma)
-modules
\mathbb{D}
over
X
;

•

a map
(\widetilde{\delta}_{1},\widetilde{\delta}_{2}):X\rightarrow\mathcal{T}^{2}
which consists of parameters for
\mathbb{D}

such that:

(1)

there is
x\in X
such that
\mathbb{D}\widehat{\otimes}k(x)=\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{rig}}^{{\dagger}}(\rho)
and

(\widetilde{\delta}_{1},\widetilde{\delta}_{2})\widehat{\otimes}k(x)=(\delta_{1},\delta_{2})
;

(2)

(\widetilde{\delta}_{1},\widetilde{\delta}_{2})=(\widetilde{\delta}_{1}^{\prime}x^{\mu_{1}},\widetilde{\delta}_{2}^{\prime}x^{\mu_{2}})
.

Proof.

Let us define the ind-rigid space
Y
to be the fiber product

(6.2)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y\coloneqq X^{\prime}\times\mathrm{Gr}^{\mathrm{rig}}_{2}\times_{\mathrm{Gr}^{\mathrm{rig}}_{2}\times\mathrm{Gr}^{\mathrm{rig}}_{2}}\mathrm{Gr}^{\mathrm{rig}}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Gr}_{2}^{\mathrm{rig}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}\times\mathrm{Gr}^{\mathrm{rig}}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Gr}^{\mathrm{rig}}_{2}\times\mathrm{Gr}^{\mathrm{rig}}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\gamma\cdot{,}\mathrm{pr})}

where the rightmost map
\delta
is the diagonal map.
Notice that for any point
((y^{\prime},\Lambda_{A}),\Lambda^{\prime}_{A})\in Y(A)
, we have

(\gamma\cdot(y^{\prime},\Lambda_{A}),\Lambda_{A})=(\Lambda_{A}^{\prime},\Lambda_{A}^{\prime})
.
Since
\Lambda
is stable under the action of
\Gamma_{m}
induced by the point
x^{\prime}
,
we have that
x\coloneqq((x^{\prime},\Lambda),\Lambda)\in Y(L)
.
We define
X
to be the fiber product

X\coloneqq Y\times_{\mathrm{Gr}^{\mathrm{rig}}_{2}}Y_{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}^{\mathrm{rig}};

we have then that
x\in X(L)
because of Lemma
6.6
and,
eventually restricting
X
, we can assume that
X
is an affinoid neighbourhood
\mathrm{Sp}(R)
of
x
.
Consider the pullback
p_{2}^{*}(\mathbb{D}^{\prime},\mathbb{L})
to
X
(where
\mathbb{L}
is the universal lattice on
\mathrm{Gr}^{\mathrm{rig}}_{2}
)
and the isomorphism of
\prod_{K_{m}\hookrightarrow L}R((t))
-modules

\widetilde{f}:p_{2}^{*}\mathbb{D}^{\prime r}[1/t]\otimes_{\mathcal{R}_{R}^{r}[1/t]}\prod_{K_{m}\hookrightarrow L}R((t))\xrightarrow{\sim}\left(\prod_{n}g_{m}^{n}\cdot p_{2}^{*}\mathbb{L}\right)[1/t]

defined as in Theorem
5.11
, where
g_{m}
is a generator of
\mathrm{Gal}(K_{m}/\mathbb{Q}_{p})

and
n
ranges over
\{0,\dots,|\mathrm{Gal}(K_{m}/\mathbb{Q}_{p})|-1\}
.
Let us denote by
\mathbb{D}
the sheaf

\left(p_{2}^{*}\mathbb{D}^{\prime},p_{2}^{*}\mathbb{L},\widetilde{f}\right)

on
X
, which can be seen as a sheaf of
(\phi,\Gamma)
-modules thanks to the equivalence
5.2
;
then we have that
\mathbb{D}\widehat{\otimes}k(x)=(D^{r}[1/t],\Lambda,f)
.
We have therefore constructed a space
X
with a sheaf
\mathbb{D}
specializing at some point
x\in X
to

(D^{r}[1/t],\Lambda,f)
, which corresponds to the
(\phi,\Gamma)
-module
\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{rig}}^{{\dagger}}(\rho)
through
the equivalence
5.2
.
Moreover the family of
(\phi,\Gamma)
-modules
\mathbb{D}
is obviously already defined over
\mathcal{R}_{X}^{r}
,
as
\mathbb{D}^{\prime}
is;
hence there is a family of
(\phi,\Gamma)
-modules
\mathbb{D}^{r}
over
\mathcal{R}_{X}^{r}
such that

\mathbb{D}=\mathbb{D}^{r}\otimes_{\mathcal{R}_{X}^{r}}\mathcal{R}_{X}
.
Notice that the sheaf of
(\phi,\Gamma)
-modules
\mathbb{D}
comes with a filtration of sub-
(\phi,\Gamma)
-modules,
which is the one induced by the filtration of sub-
(\phi,\Gamma)
-modules of
\mathbb{D}^{\prime}
.
More precisely, for any affinoid
L
-algebra
A
and for
y=((y^{\prime},\Lambda_{A}),\Lambda_{A})\in X(A)
, we have

\mathrm{Fil}^{i}(y^{*}\mathbb{D})=y^{*}\mathbb{D}\cap\mathrm{Fil}^{i}(y^{\prime*}\mathbb{D}^{\prime}[1/t]).

Recall that
\mathbb{D}^{\prime}[1/t]=\mathbb{D}[1/t]
by construction, so the intersection above makes sense.
Moreover notice that
\mathrm{Fil}^{i}(y^{*}\mathbb{D})
is stable under
\phi
and
\Gamma
,
since both
y^{*}\mathbb{D}
and
\mathrm{Fil}^{i}(y^{\prime*}\mathbb{D}^{\prime}[1/t])
are.
Finally we have that
\mathrm{Fil}^{i}(y^{*}\mathbb{D})
is locally free by Lemma
5.20
.
Therefore
\mathrm{Fil}^{\bullet}(y^{*}\mathbb{D})
provides a triangulation of
\mathbb{D}

and hence it gives parameters
(\widetilde{\delta}_{1},\widetilde{\delta}_{2})\in\mathcal{T}^{2}(X)
for the family
\mathbb{D}
.
The last thing left to show is that
(\widetilde{\delta}_{1},\widetilde{\delta}_{2})=(\widetilde{\delta}_{1}^{\prime}x^{\mu_{1}},\widetilde{\delta}_{2}^{\prime}x^{\mu_{2}})
.
We claim that

\mathrm{gr}^{1}(p_{2}^{*}\mathbb{L})=\mathcal{R}_{R}^{r}(\widetilde{\delta}_{1})\otimes_{\mathcal{R}_{R}^{r},\tau}R\llbracket t\rrbracket

and

\mathrm{gr}^{2}(p_{2}^{*}\mathbb{L})=\mathcal{R}_{R}^{r}(\widetilde{\delta}_{2})\otimes_{\mathcal{R}_{R}^{r},\tau}R\llbracket t\rrbracket.

Let
q:X\rightarrow X^{\prime}
be the projection map; since we fixed a basis
of the standard lattice
\mathbb{L}^{\prime}
over
R^{\prime}
respecting
the triangulation of
\mathbb{D}^{\prime}
, we have that

\displaystyle\mathrm{Fil}^{1}(R((t))^{2})

\displaystyle=q^{*}\mathrm{Fil}^{1}(R^{\prime}((t))^{2})=q^{*}(\mathcal{R}_{R^{\prime}}^{r}(\widetilde{\delta}_{1}^{\prime})[1/t]\otimes_{\mathcal{R}_{R^{\prime}}^{r}[1/t],\tau}R^{\prime}((t)))

\displaystyle=\mathcal{R}_{R}^{r}(\widetilde{\delta}_{1}^{\prime})[1/t]\otimes_{\mathcal{R}_{R}^{r}[1/t],\tau}R((t)).

Thus

\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}^{1}(p_{2}^{*}\mathbb{L})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Fil}^{1}(p_{2}^{*}\mathbb{L})=p_{2}^{*}\mathbb{L}\cap\mathrm{Fil}^{1}(R((t))^{2})

\displaystyle=\left(\mathbb{D}^{r}\otimes_{\mathcal{R}_{R}^{r},\tau}R\llbracket t\rrbracket\right)\cap\left(\mathcal{R}_{R}^{r}(\widetilde{\delta}_{1}^{\prime})[1/t]\otimes_{\mathcal{R}_{R}^{r}[1/t],\tau}R((t))\right)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{R}_{R}^{r}(\widetilde{\delta}_{1})\otimes_{\mathcal{R}_{R}^{r},\tau}R\llbracket t\rrbracket

and

\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}^{2}(p_{2}^{*}\mathbb{L})

\displaystyle=p_{2}^{*}\mathbb{L}/\mathrm{Fil}^{1}(p_{2}^{*}\mathbb{L})=\left(\mathbb{D}^{r}\otimes_{\mathcal{R}_{R}^{r},\tau}R\llbracket t\rrbracket\right)/\left(\mathcal{R}_{R}^{r}(\widetilde{\delta}_{1})\otimes_{\mathcal{R}_{R}^{r},\tau}R\llbracket t\rrbracket\right)

\displaystyle\cong\mathcal{R}_{R}^{r}(\widetilde{\delta}_{2})\otimes_{\mathcal{R}_{R}^{r},\tau}R\llbracket t\rrbracket.

Restricting ourselves to lattices in
Y_{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}
as in the definition of the space
X
, we have

\mathcal{R}_{R}^{r}(\widetilde{\delta}_{1})\otimes_{\mathcal{R}_{R}^{r},\tau}R\llbracket t\rrbracket=t^{\mu_{1}}\mathcal{R}_{R}^{r}(\widetilde{\delta}_{1}^{\prime})\otimes_{\mathcal{R}_{R}^{r},\tau}R\llbracket t\rrbracket=\mathcal{R}_{R}^{r}(x^{\mu_{1}}\widetilde{\delta}_{1}^{\prime})\otimes_{\mathcal{R}_{R}^{r},\tau}R\llbracket t\rrbracket

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{R}^{r}(\widetilde{\delta}_{2})\otimes_{\mathcal{R}_{R}^{r},\tau}R\llbracket t\rrbracket

\displaystyle=t^{\mu_{2}}\cdot\mathcal{R}_{R}^{r}(\widetilde{\delta}_{2}^{\prime})\otimes_{\mathcal{R}_{R}^{r},\tau}R\llbracket t\rrbracket=\mathcal{R}_{R}^{r}(x^{\mu_{2}}\widetilde{\delta}_{2}^{\prime})\otimes_{\mathcal{R}_{R}^{r},\tau}R\llbracket t\rrbracket,

which concludes the proof.
∎

We have then shown that there is a rigid space
X
and a family of
(\phi,\Gamma)
-modules

\mathbb{D}
such that
\mathbb{D}\widehat{\otimes}k(x)\cong\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{rig}}^{{\dagger}}(\rho)
at some point
x\in X(L)
.
The only thing left to show is that the sheaf of
(\phi,\Gamma)
-modules

\mathbb{D}
is regular in a dense subspace of a neighbourhood of the point
x\in X(L)
.
In order to do so, we must argue in a different way from section 4, since the rigid space
X
is
not a vector bundle over an appropriate neighbourhood
\mathcal{U}
of
(\delta_{1},\delta_{2})
in
\mathcal{T}^{2}
.
As a consequence, we cannot conclude that
(\widetilde{\delta}_{1},\widetilde{\delta}_{2})^{-1}(\mathcal{U}\cap\mathcal{T}^{\mathrm{reg}}_{2})

is dense in
X
, as the map
X\rightarrow\mathcal{T}^{2}
is not necessarily open.

6.2.
Density of the regular locus

To shorten the notation, let us denote by
G
the map

(\widetilde{\delta}_{1},\widetilde{\delta}_{2}):X\rightarrow\mathcal{T}^{2}
sending a point
y
of
X
to
the parameters associated to the filtration of
y^{*}\mathbb{D}
.
Moreover, let

H:\mathcal{T}^{2}\rightarrow\mathcal{W}^{2},(\widetilde{\eta}_{1},\widetilde{\eta}_{2})\mapsto(\widetilde{\eta}_{1|\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}},\widetilde{\eta}_{2|\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}})

be the map sending continuous characters of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times}
to their restriction to
\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}
.
Finally, Let

(6.3)

F:X\xrightarrow{G}\mathcal{T}^{2}\xrightarrow{H}\mathcal{W}^{2}

be the composition of the maps
G
and
H
.

Lemma 6.9
.

If
(\widetilde{\eta}_{1},\widetilde{\eta}_{2})\in\mathcal{T}^{2}(K)\cap\mathrm{im}(G)
for some extension
K
of
L
, then

\dim(G^{-1}(\widetilde{\eta}_{1},\widetilde{\eta}_{2}))=\dim_{K}H^{1}_{\phi,\Gamma}(\mathcal{R}_{K}(\widetilde{\eta}_{1}/\widetilde{\eta}_{2})).

Proof.

We have a map

(6.4)

G^{-1}(\widetilde{\eta}_{1},\widetilde{\eta}_{2})=\{y\in X(K)\colon\mathbb{D}\widehat{\otimes}k(y)\in H^{1}_{\phi,\Gamma}(\mathcal{R}_{K}(\widetilde{\eta}_{1}/\widetilde{\eta}_{2}))\}\rightarrow H^{1}_{\phi,\Gamma}(\mathcal{R}_{K}(\widetilde{\eta}_{1}/\widetilde{\eta}_{2}))

via
y\mapsto\mathbb{D}\widehat{\otimes}k(y)
.
In order to show the identity of dimensions, we must prove that the map is bijective.
We start with surjectivity: let
E\in H^{1}_{\phi,\Gamma}(\mathcal{R}_{K}(\widetilde{\eta}_{1}/\widetilde{\eta}_{2}))
,
then we have that (by construction of
X^{\prime}
and of the family
\mathbb{D}^{\prime}
) there is
y^{\prime}\in X^{\prime}(K)
such that

(\mathbb{D}^{\prime}\widehat{\otimes}k(y^{\prime}))[1/t]=E[1/t]\in H^{1}_{\phi,\Gamma}(\mathcal{R}_{K}(\widetilde{\eta}_{1}/\widetilde{\eta}_{2}\cdot x^{-n-1}))
.
We still need to show that the lattice
\Lambda_{y}
corresponding to the factor
\tau:K_{m}\hookrightarrow L

of the
\prod_{K_{m}\hookrightarrow L}K\llbracket t\rrbracket
-module
associated to
E
has the right graded pieces; in other words we want

\Lambda_{y}\coloneqq E^{r}\otimes_{\mathcal{R}_{K}^{r},\tau}K\llbracket t\rrbracket\in Y_{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}(K),

where
E^{r}
is the
(\phi,\Gamma)
-module over
\mathcal{R}_{K}^{r}
such that
E=E^{r}\otimes_{\mathcal{R}_{K}^{r}}\mathcal{R}_{K}
.
This
(\phi,\Gamma)
-module exists, since the whole family
\mathbb{D}^{\prime}
is defined over
\mathcal{R}_{X^{\prime}}^{r}
.
We have that

\displaystyle\mathrm{Fil}^{1}(K((t))^{2})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Fil}^{1}(\mathbb{L}^{\prime}[1/t])\otimes k(y^{\prime})=(\mathcal{R}_{R^{\prime}}^{r}(\widetilde{\delta}_{1}^{\prime})[1/t]\otimes_{\mathcal{R}_{R^{\prime}}^{r}[1/t],\tau}R^{\prime}((t)))\otimes k(y^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{R}_{K}^{r}(\widetilde{\eta}_{1}x^{-\mu_{1}})[1/t]\otimes_{\mathcal{R}^{r}_{K}[1/t],\tau}K((t)),

so

\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}^{1}(\Lambda_{y})

\displaystyle=\Lambda_{y}\cap\mathrm{Fil}^{1}(K((t))^{2})

\displaystyle=\left(E^{r}\otimes_{\mathcal{R}_{K}^{r},\tau}K\llbracket t\rrbracket\right)\cap\left(\mathcal{R}_{K}^{r}(\widetilde{\eta}_{1}x^{-\mu_{1}})[1/t]\otimes_{\mathcal{R}^{r}_{K}[1/t],\tau}K((t))\right)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{R}_{K}^{r}(\widetilde{\eta}_{1})\otimes_{\mathcal{R}^{r}_{K},\tau}K\llbracket t\rrbracket=t^{\mu_{1}}\mathrm{gr}^{1}(\mathbb{L}^{\prime}\otimes k(y^{\prime}))

and

\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}^{2}(\Lambda_{y})

\displaystyle=\left(E^{r}\otimes_{\mathcal{R}_{K}^{r},\tau}K\llbracket t\rrbracket\right)/\left(\mathcal{R}_{K}^{r}(\widetilde{\eta}_{1})\otimes_{\mathcal{R}^{r}_{K},\tau}K\llbracket t\rrbracket\right)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{R}_{K}^{r}(\widetilde{\eta}_{2})\otimes_{\mathcal{R}^{r}_{K},\tau}K\llbracket t\rrbracket=t^{\mu_{2}}\mathcal{R}_{K}^{r}(\widetilde{\eta}_{2}\cdot x^{-\mu_{2}})\otimes_{\mathcal{R}^{r}_{K},\tau}K\llbracket t\rrbracket

\displaystyle=t^{\mu_{2}}\mathrm{gr}^{2}(\mathbb{L}^{\prime}\otimes k(y^{\prime})).

Hence we have
y\coloneqq(y^{\prime},\Lambda_{y})\in X(K)
and
\mathbb{D}\widehat{\otimes}k(y)=E
.
This proves the surjectivity of the above map.
Now suppose by contradiction that
\mathbb{D}\widehat{\otimes}k(y_{1})=\mathbb{D}\widehat{\otimes}k(y_{2})=E\in H^{1}_{\phi,\Gamma}(\mathcal{R}_{K}(\widetilde{\eta}_{1}/\widetilde{\eta}_{2}))

for some
y_{1},y_{2}\in X(K)
.
In particular we have
y_{1}=(y_{1}^{\prime},\Lambda_{y})
and
y_{2}=(y_{2}^{\prime},\Lambda_{y})
for some
y_{1}^{\prime},y_{2}^{\prime}\in X^{\prime}(K)

such that
(\mathbb{D}^{\prime}\widehat{\otimes}k(y_{1}^{\prime}))[1/t]=(\mathbb{D}^{\prime}\widehat{\otimes}k(y_{2}^{\prime}))[1/t]=E[1/t]

and
\Lambda_{y}\coloneqq E^{r}\otimes_{\mathcal{R}_{K}^{r},\tau}K\llbracket t\rrbracket
.
But since the map

\displaystyle\Psi_{K}:X^{\prime}(K)

\displaystyle\rightarrow H^{1}_{\phi,\Gamma}(\mathcal{R}_{K}(\widetilde{\eta}_{1}/\widetilde{\eta}_{2}\cdot x^{-n-1}))

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle\mapsto f^{*}\mathbb{D}^{\prime}

is bijective (as explained in Remark
6.5
), we have
y_{1}^{\prime}=y_{2}^{\prime}
.
This shows injectivity and concludes the proof.
∎

Theorem 6.10
.

The map

F:X\xrightarrow{G}\mathcal{T}^{2}\xrightarrow{H}\mathcal{W}^{2}

has equidimensional fibers of dimension 3.

Proof.

Let
(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})\in\mathcal{W}^{2}(K)\cap\mathrm{im}(F)
and let us study the fiber
F^{-1}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})
.
We have that

\dim H^{-1}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})=2,

which is easy to see thanks to the identification
\mathcal{T}\cong\mathcal{W}\times\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\mathrm{an}}
.
Now we want to consider two different cases:

(1)

If
\eta_{1}/\eta_{2}\notin x^{\mathbb{N}_{>0}}
, then notice that
H^{-1}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})\cap\mathrm{im}(G)\subset\mathcal{T}^{\mathrm{reg}}_{2}(K)
:
if
\eta_{1}/\eta_{2}\notin x^{\mathbb{Z}}
, then it is obvious that
H^{-1}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})\subset\mathcal{T}^{\mathrm{reg}}_{2}(K)
;
on the other hand, if
\eta_{1}/\eta_{2}=x^{-n}
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
, then
notice that

\{(\widetilde{\eta}_{1},\widetilde{\eta}_{2})\in H^{-1}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})\colon\widetilde{\eta}_{1}/\widetilde{\eta}_{2}=x^{-n}\}\cap\mathrm{im}(G)=\emptyset:

in fact we chose
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{T}^{\mathrm{reg}}_{2}
and we have

\mathrm{im}(G)=\mathcal{U}=\{(\widetilde{\delta}_{1}^{\prime},\widetilde{\delta}_{2}^{\prime}\cdot x^{-n-1})\colon(\widetilde{\delta}_{1}^{\prime},\widetilde{\delta}_{2}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{U}^{\prime}\}.

It is then easy to see that the above implies that

\mathrm{im}(G)\cap\{(\widetilde{\eta}_{1},\widetilde{\eta}_{2})\colon\widetilde{\eta}_{1}/\widetilde{\eta}_{2}\in x^{-\mathbb{N}}\}=\emptyset.

Let
(\widetilde{\eta}_{1},\widetilde{\eta}_{2})\in H^{-1}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})\cap\mathrm{im}(G)
;
by Lemma
6.9
, we have

\dim G^{-1}(\widetilde{\eta}_{1},\widetilde{\eta}_{2})=\dim_{K}H^{1}_{\phi,\Gamma}(\mathcal{R}_{K}(\widetilde{\eta}_{1}/\widetilde{\eta}_{2}))=1.

The last identity follows from the fact that
(\widetilde{\eta}_{1},\widetilde{\eta}_{2})\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathrm{reg}}_{2}(K)
.
Therefore,

\dim F^{-1}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})=3.

(2)

In the case in which
\eta_{1}/\eta_{2}=x^{n}
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}
,
it is convenient to partition the space
H^{-1}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})
in the following way:

H^{-1}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})=(H^{-1}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})\cap\mathcal{T}^{\mathrm{reg}}_{2})\dot{\cup}\{(\widetilde{\eta}_{1},\widetilde{\eta}_{2})\in H^{-1}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})\colon\widetilde{\eta}_{1}/\widetilde{\eta}_{2}=\chi x^{n-1}\}.

Let us denote by
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
respectively the first and second subspace.

•

We have that, as
\mathcal{T}^{\mathrm{reg}}_{2}
is open in
\mathcal{T}^{2}

\dim\mathcal{A}=\dim H^{-1}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})=2.

Let
(\widetilde{\eta}_{1},\widetilde{\eta}_{2})\in\mathcal{A}\cap\mathrm{im}(G)
;

\dim G^{-1}(\widetilde{\eta}_{1},\widetilde{\eta}_{2})=\dim_{K}H^{1}_{\phi,\Gamma}(\mathcal{R}_{K}(\widetilde{\eta}_{1}/\widetilde{\eta}_{2}))=1.

•

For a point
(\widetilde{\eta}_{1},\widetilde{\eta}_{2})\in\mathcal{B}\cap\mathrm{im}(G)
, we have

\dim G^{-1}(\widetilde{\eta}_{1},\widetilde{\eta}_{2})=\dim_{K}H^{1}_{\phi,\Gamma}(\mathcal{R}_{K}(\widetilde{\eta}_{1}/\widetilde{\eta}_{2}))=2.

Finally, (through the identification
\mathcal{T}\cong\mathcal{W}\times\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\mathrm{an}}
) we have

\displaystyle\dim\mathcal{B}

\displaystyle=\dim(\{(\widetilde{\eta}_{1},\widetilde{\eta}_{2})\in H^{-1}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})\colon\widetilde{\eta}_{1}/\widetilde{\eta}_{2}=\chi x^{n-1}\})

\displaystyle=\dim(\{(\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\widetilde{\eta}_{1}(p),\widetilde{\eta}_{2}(p))\in\mathcal{W}^{2}\times\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\mathrm{an},2}\colon\widetilde{\eta}_{1}(p)/\widetilde{\eta}_{2}(p)=p^{n-1}\})

\displaystyle=1.

Therefore we have

\dim F^{-1}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})=\dim\mathcal{A}+1=\dim\mathcal{B}+2=3.

∎

Remark 6.11
.

(i)

From the proof of the previous Theorem, we can already notce that the fibers of the
map
G
are not equidimensional, hence in particular
G
is not flat.

(ii)

Moreover we have that

\dim(F^{-1}(\mathcal{W}^{\mathrm{reg}}_{2}))=3+\dim\mathcal{W}^{\mathrm{reg}}_{2}=5=\dim\mathcal{U}^{\prime}+1=\dim X^{\prime},

since
X^{\prime}
is a vector bundle of dimension
\dim_{L}H^{1}_{\phi,\Gamma}(\mathcal{R}_{L}(\delta_{1}/\delta_{2}^{\prime}))=1

over
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
, as proved in Proposition
6.4
.

From now on we will study the geometry of the space
X
, in order to conclude from the
bound of the fiber dimension that
F^{-1}(\mathcal{W}_{2}^{\mathrm{reg}})
is dense in
X
.

Lemma 6.12
.

Let
K
be an extension of
L
and let
A
be an Artin local algebra with residue field
K
.
Let
\Lambda
be a free
K\llbracket t\rrbracket
-module endowed with an action of
\Gamma_{m}

and let
\Lambda_{A}
be a free
A\llbracket t\rrbracket
-module endowed with an action of
\Gamma_{m}

such that
\Lambda_{A}\otimes_{A}K=\Lambda
.
Then we have that the Sen weights of
\Lambda
(viewed as a representation with
L
-coefficients)
and
\Lambda_{A}
are the same.

Proof.

For simplicity let us denote by
\overline{\Lambda}_{A}
the
A
-module
\Lambda_{A}/t\Lambda_{A}

and we show that it is a successive extension of
\overline{\Lambda}\coloneqq\Lambda/t\Lambda
.
In fact, if we let
\mathfrak{m}_{A}
denote the maximal ideal of
A
, then the module

\overline{\Lambda}_{A}
has a filtration

\overline{\Lambda}_{A}\supset\mathfrak{m}_{A}\overline{\Lambda}_{A}\supset\dots\supset\mathfrak{m}_{A}^{l}\overline{\Lambda}_{A}\supset 0,

where
l\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\mathfrak{m}_{A}^{l+1}=0
, which exists as
A
is Artinian.
For
1\leqslant i\leqslant l
, let
k\coloneqq\dim_{K}\mathfrak{m}_{A}^{i}/\mathfrak{m}_{A}^{i+1}
and let us fix a basis

\{m_{1},\dots,m_{k}\}
of
\mathfrak{m}_{A}^{i}/\mathfrak{m}_{A}^{i+1}
over
K
.
Then we have an isomorphism of
K
-vector spaces

(6.5)

\mathfrak{m}_{A}^{i}\overline{\Lambda}_{A}/\mathfrak{m}_{A}^{i+1}\overline{\Lambda}_{A}\cong\mathfrak{m}_{A}^{i}/\mathfrak{m}_{A}^{i+1}\otimes_{A}\overline{\Lambda}_{A}\xrightarrow{\sim}\bigoplus_{j=1}^{k}\overline{\Lambda},

where for every
m=\sum_{i=1}^{k}a_{i}m_{i}\in\mathfrak{m}_{A}^{i}/\mathfrak{m}_{A}^{i+1}

and every
l\in\overline{\Lambda}_{A}
, we have

m\otimes l\mapsto(a_{i}(l+\mathfrak{m}_{A})+t\Lambda)_{i=1,\dots,k}.

Observe moreover that
\overline{\Lambda}
and
\overline{\Lambda}_{A}
inherit
the
\Gamma_{m}
-action of
\Lambda
and
\Lambda_{A}
and that the isomorphism (
6.5
)
is
\Gamma_{m}
-equivariant.
Notice furthermore that the
\Gamma_{m}
-action on
\overline{\Lambda}_{A}
preserves the filtration,
thus we have that, once we have chosen an appropriate basis of
\overline{\Lambda}_{A}
,
the matrix of the Sen operator is an upper triangular block matrix with diagonal blocks the
matrix of the Sen operator on
\overline{\Lambda}
.
This concludes the proof, as the Sen weights are the eigenvalues of the Sen operator.
∎

Remark 6.13
.

The same proof as Lemma
6.12
shows that if
W^{+}
is a
B_{\mathrm{dR},K}
-representation
of
G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
and
W_{A}^{+}
is a
B_{\mathrm{dR},A}^{+}
-representation of
G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
such that

W_{A}^{+}\otimes_{A}K=W^{+}
, then
W_{A}^{+}
and
W^{+}
have the same Sen weights.

The following results will be useful also in the
n
-dimensional case for
n>2
, so we write
the proof for the
n
-dimensional case directly here.
Note that in the following, in case
n=2
we have
X_{n}
is simply
X
,

X_{n}^{\prime}
is
X^{\prime}
,
\mathbb{D}_{n}
is
\mathbb{D}
and
\mathbb{D}_{n}^{\prime}
is
\mathbb{D}^{\prime}
.

Remark 6.14
.

Let us consider the stalk

\mathrm{Sp}(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{n},x_{n}})

and for simplicity let us denote by
\mathbb{D}_{n}
and
\mathbb{D}_{n}^{\prime}
the pullback of
\mathbb{D}_{n}
and
\mathbb{D}_{n}^{\prime}
to the stalk.
Let
\widehat{\Lambda}\coloneqq\mathbb{D}_{n}^{r}\otimes_{\mathcal{R}_{R}^{r},\tau}R\llbracket t\rrbracket

and
\widehat{\Lambda}^{\prime}\coloneqq\mathbb{D}_{n}^{\prime r}\otimes_{\mathcal{R}_{R}^{r},\tau}R\llbracket t\rrbracket
, where

R\coloneqq\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{n},x_{n}}
.
Observe that
\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{n},x_{n}}\varprojlim_{m}\mathcal{O}_{X_{n},x_{n}}/\mathfrak{m}^{m}
,
where
\mathfrak{m}
is the maximal ideal associated to the point
x_{n}
.
Let us consider the maps

\mathrm{Spec}(O_{X_{n},x_{n}}/\mathfrak{m}^{m})\xrightarrow{q_{m}}\mathrm{Spf}(\widehat{O}_{X_{n},x_{n}}).

The ring
O_{X_{n},x_{n}}/\mathfrak{m}^{m}
is a finite-dimensional Artin local
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-algebra for all
m\geqslant 1

hence by Lemma
6.12
,
q_{m}^{*}\widehat{\Lambda}^{\prime}

has the same Sen weights as
\Lambda^{\prime}
.
Notice that by construction, if
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
are two Sen weights of
q_{m}^{*}\widehat{\Lambda}^{\prime}

and
a_{1}\equiv a_{2}\pmod{\mathbb{Z}}
, then
a_{1}=a_{2}
; moreover observe that if
a_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}
, then
a_{1}=0
.
Therefore if we let
\mathcal{A}
be the set of Sen weights of
\Lambda^{\prime}
,
we have that the lattice
q_{m}^{*}\widehat{\Lambda}^{\prime}
decomposes as explained in Remark
5.46

as

q_{m}^{*}\widehat{\Lambda}^{\prime}=\bigoplus_{a\in\mathcal{A}}(q_{m}^{*}\widehat{\Lambda}^{\prime})_{a},

where
(q_{m}^{*}\widehat{\Lambda}^{\prime})_{a}
is a
\Gamma_{m}
-stable sublattice for all
a\in\mathcal{A}
.
In this way we obtain a decomposition of
\widehat{\Lambda}^{\prime}
:

\widehat{\Lambda}^{\prime}=\bigoplus_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\widehat{\Lambda}^{\prime}_{a},

where
\widehat{\Lambda}^{\prime}_{a}
is a
\Gamma_{m}
-stable sublattice.
Moreover
\widehat{\Lambda}
and
\widehat{\Lambda}^{\prime}
have the same
Sen weights modulo
\mathbb{Z}
by Lemma
5.43
.
Thus in exactly the same manner we obtain a decomposition

\widehat{\Lambda}=\bigoplus_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\widehat{\Lambda}_{a},

where each
\widehat{\Lambda}_{a}
is a
\Gamma_{m}
-stable
R\llbracket t\rrbracket
-lattice.
For each
a\in\mathcal{A}
, let
\delta_{a}
be a character of weight
a
if
a\neq 0

and let
\delta_{a}
be the trivial character when
a=0
.
Then after choosing an appropriate basis of
\widehat{\Lambda}_{a}^{\prime}(\delta_{a}^{-1})
,
the matrix of
\gamma-1
is upper triangular with zero on the diagonal, where
\gamma
is a
topological generator of
\Gamma_{m}
.
As a consequence, we have that

(\widehat{\Lambda}_{a}^{\prime}[1/t](\delta_{a}^{-1}))^{(\gamma-1)\text{-nil}}\otimes_{R}R\llbracket t\rrbracket=\widehat{\Lambda}_{a}^{\prime}(\delta_{a}^{-1})
.
In other words,
\widehat{\Lambda}^{\prime}
is the lattice
\Lambda_{0}
constructed in Remark
5.46

and the basis
\{y_{\bar{a},1},\dots,y_{\bar{a},n_{\bar{a}}\colon\bar{a}\in\bar{\mathcal{A}}}\}

defined in the Remark respects the triangulation of
\mathbb{D}^{\prime}
(up to reordering), hence we can
choose it as the standard basis of
\widehat{\Lambda}^{\prime}
.
Moreover
\widehat{\Lambda}_{a}[1/t]=\widehat{\Lambda}_{a}^{\prime}[1/t]
for all
\bar{a}\in\mathcal{A}
.

From now on, let us denote by
Y_{\mu}
the space
Y_{(\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{n})}
for simplicity of notation.
Let us decompose
\Lambda
and
\Lambda^{\prime}
as in Remark
5.46
:
let
\mathcal{A}
be the set of Sen weights of the
\Gamma_{m}
-stable lattice
\Lambda^{\prime}

(which are the same as the mod
\mathbb{Z}
classes of the Sen weights of
\Lambda

by Lemma
5.43
); then we have

\begin{array}[]{l l l}\Lambda=\bigoplus_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\Lambda_{a}&\text{ and }&\Lambda^{\prime}=\bigoplus_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}\Lambda^{\prime}_{a},\end{array}

where
\Lambda_{a}
and
\Lambda_{a}^{\prime}
are
\Gamma_{m}
-stable sub-
L\llbracket t\rrbracket
-modules
of the same rank
n_{a}
. After fixing
\Lambda_{a}^{\prime}
to be the standard lattice of
\mathrm{Gr}^{\mathrm{rig}}_{n_{a}}(L)
, let

\mu_{a}\in X_{*}(T_{n_{a}})^{+}
such that
\Lambda_{a}\in\mathrm{Gr}_{n_{a}}^{\mu_{a},\mathrm{rig}}
.
Moreover let
\lambda_{a}\in X_{*}(T_{n_{a}})
such that

Y_{\mu}\times_{\mathrm{Gr}_{n}}\prod_{a\in\mathcal{A}}GL_{n_{a}}/P_{\mu_{a}}=\prod_{a\in\mathcal{A}}Y_{\lambda_{a}}\times_{\mathrm{Gr}_{n_{a}}}GL_{n_{a}}/P_{\mu_{a}}

(which is possible by Lemma
5.23
)
and let
w_{a}
in the Weyl group of
GL_{n_{a}}
such that
\lambda_{a}=w_{a}w_{0}\mu_{a}
for all
a\in\mathcal{A}
.

Lemma 6.15
.

There exists a commutative diagram

(6.6)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Spf}(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{n},x_{n}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Spf}(\widehat{R})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Spf}(\widehat{S})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Spf}(\widehat{T}),}

where

\displaystyle R

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathcal{O}_{X_{n}^{\prime}\times Y_{\mu}^{\mathrm{rig}}\times_{\mathrm{Gr}^{\mathrm{rig}}_{n}}\prod_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}(GL_{n_{a}}/P_{\mu_{a}})^{\mathrm{rig}},(x_{n},(\pi_{\mu_{a}}(\Lambda_{a}))_{a\in\mathcal{A}})},

\displaystyle S

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathcal{O}_{\prod_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}V_{w_{a}}^{\mu_{a}},(N_{a},\pi_{\mu_{a}}(\Lambda_{a}))_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}},

\displaystyle T

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathcal{O}_{\prod_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}\mathfrak{b}_{n_{a}}^{\mathrm{rig}}\times(B_{n}w_{a}P_{\mu_{a}}/P_{\mu_{a}})^{\mathrm{rig}},(N_{a},\pi_{\mu_{a}}(\Lambda_{a}))_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}}.

In the above
\mathfrak{b}_{n_{a}}
is the Lie algebra of the Borel of upper
triangular matrices
B_{n_{a}}
, while the space
V_{w_{a}}^{\mu_{a}}
is defined in (
5.12
).
Finally
N_{a}
is the matrix of the nilpotent endomorphism acting on

D_{\mathrm{pdR},a}(W_{\mathrm{dR}}(x_{n}^{*}\mathbb{D}_{n}[1/t])_{a})

for all
a\in{\mathcal{A}}
, after having chosen a basis of
D_{\mathrm{pdR},a}(W_{\mathrm{dR}}(x_{n}^{*}\mathbb{D}_{n}[1/t])_{a})
.
Observe that if we choose a basis of
D_{\mathrm{pdR},a}(W_{\mathrm{dR}}(x_{n}^{*}\mathbb{D}_{n}[1/t])_{a})

respecting the triangulation of
x_{n}^{*}\mathbb{D}_{n}
,
then we have that the matrix
N_{a}
is upper triangular; hence we have
N_{a}\in\mathfrak{b}_{n_{a}}

for all
a\in\mathcal{A}
.

Proof.

Recall that for a rigid space
X
and a point
x\in X(K)
, we have that
\mathrm{Spf}(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{X,x})

prorepresents the functor

\displaystyle\{\text{Artin local }K\text{-algebras with residue field }K\}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\underline{Sets}

sending an Artin local
K
-algebra
A
with maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}

to the set of morphisms
\mathrm{Sp}(A)\xrightarrow{f}X
such that
the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Sp}(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Sp}(A/\mathfrak{m})=\mathrm{Sp}(K)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}

commutes (in fact if
K
is a complete non-Archimedean valued field, then Artin
local
K
-algebras are affinoid
K
-algebras).
Let
\widehat{\Lambda}
be the lattice associated to
\hat{x}_{n}^{*}\mathbb{D}_{n}
, where

\hat{x}_{n}:\mathrm{Sp}(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{n},x_{n}})\rightarrow X_{n}
.
As explained in Remark
6.14
, we get a decomposition

\widehat{\Lambda}=\bigoplus_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\widehat{\Lambda}_{a},

where
\mathcal{A}
is the set of Sen weights of
\Lambda^{\prime}
and
\widehat{\Lambda}_{a}
is a

\Gamma_{m}
-stable lattice of rank
n_{a}
.
First of all notice that the map

(\pi)_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}:\mathrm{Sp}(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{n},x_{n}})\rightarrow\prod_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}\coprod_{\lambda_{a}\in X_{*}(T)^{+}}(GL_{n_{a}}/P_{\lambda_{a}})^{\mathrm{rig}}

given by
(\pi(\widehat{\Lambda}_{a}))_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}

factors through
\prod_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}(GL_{n_{a}}/P_{\mu_{a}})^{\mathrm{rig}}

(where
(GL_{n_{a}}/P_{\lambda_{a}})^{\mathrm{rig}}

is the rigid analytification of the
L
-scheme of locally finite type
GL_{n_{a}}/P_{\lambda_{a}}
):
in fact we have that

(\pi)_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}(x_{n})\in\prod_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}(GL_{n_{a}}/P_{\mu_{a}})^{\mathrm{rig}}

and
\mathrm{Sp}(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{n},x_{n}})
is connected.
The upper horizontal map of the diagram (
6.6
) is defined then by the commutative diagram

(6.7)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Sp}(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{n},x_{n}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{n}^{\prime}\times Y_{\mu}^{\mathrm{rig}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}(GL_{n_{a}}/P_{\mu_{a}})^{\mathrm{rig}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Gr}^{\mathrm{rig}}_{n},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{x}_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\pi)_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oplus_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}\sigma_{\mu_{a}}}

which gives a map

(6.8)

(\hat{x}_{n},(\pi)_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}):\mathrm{Sp}(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{n},x_{n}})\rightarrow X_{n}^{\prime}\times Y_{\mu}^{\mathrm{rig}}\times_{\mathrm{Gr}^{\mathrm{rig}}_{n}}\prod_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}(GL_{n_{a}}/P_{\mu_{a}})^{\mathrm{rig}}.

Here by
\oplus_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}\sigma_{\mu_{a}}
we mean the map

(\mathrm{Fil}^{\bullet}(\Lambda^{\prime}_{a}/t\Lambda^{\prime}_{a}))_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}\mapsto\bigoplus_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}\sigma_{\mu_{a}}(\mathrm{Fil}^{\bullet}(\Lambda^{\prime}_{a}/t\Lambda^{\prime}_{a})).

In fact the diagram (
6.7
) is commutative:
since
\widehat{\Lambda}_{a}
is
\Gamma_{m}
-stable,
by Lemma
5.49
we have that

\widehat{\Lambda}=(\oplus_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}\sigma_{\mu_{a}})\circ((\pi)_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}})(\widehat{\Lambda}),

thus the diagram commutes.
It is obvious that

(\hat{x}_{n},(\pi)_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}})\circ x_{n}=(x_{n},(\pi_{\mu_{a}}(\Lambda_{a}))_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}).

The lower horizontal map of (
6.6
) is defined by the composition

i:\mathrm{Sp}(\widehat{S})\rightarrow\prod_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}V_{w_{a}}^{\mu_{a}}\hookrightarrow\prod_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}\mathfrak{b}_{n_{a}}^{\mathrm{rig}}\times(GL_{n_{a}}/P_{\mu_{a}})^{\mathrm{rig}},

where
\mathfrak{b}_{n_{a}}^{\mathrm{rig}}
is the rigid analytification of
\mathfrak{b}_{n_{a}}
;
it is again obvious that

i\circ(N_{a},\pi_{\mu_{a}}(\Lambda_{a}))_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}=(N_{a},\pi_{\mu_{a}}(\Lambda_{a}))_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}.

As for the left vertical arrow,
let us consider again the maps

\displaystyle\mathrm{Sp}(\mathcal{O}_{X_{n},x_{n}}/\mathfrak{m}^{m})\xrightarrow{q_{m}}\mathrm{Sp}(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{n},x_{n}})\xrightarrow{\hat{x}_{n}}X_{n}

and the pullbacks
q_{m}^{*}\hat{x}_{n}^{*}\mathbb{D}_{n}
.
We have that
\mathcal{O}_{X_{n},x_{n}}/\mathfrak{m}^{m}
is a finite dimensional
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-algebra and by
Remark
6.13
we have that
W_{\mathrm{dR}}^{+}(q_{m}^{*}\hat{x}_{n}^{*}\mathbb{D}_{n})
has the same Sen weights as
W_{\mathrm{dR}}^{+}(x_{n}^{*}\mathbb{D}_{n})
.
So there are maps

(\widetilde{N}_{m,a},\pi_{\mu_{a}})_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}:\mathrm{Sp}(\mathcal{O}_{X_{n},x_{n}}/\mathfrak{m}^{m})\rightarrow\prod_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}\mathrm{Fil}^{\mathrm{st},\mathrm{rig}}_{\mu_{\bar{a}}},

which are determined by
the
\widetilde{N}_{m,a}
-stable filtrations associated to the lattice

q_{m}^{*}\widehat{\Lambda}

through the bijection of Theorem
5.50
.
As we have already remarked, after choosing an appropriate basis of

D_{\mathrm{pdR},a}(W_{\mathrm{dR}}(q_{m}^{*}x_{n}^{*}\mathbb{D}_{n}[1/t])_{a})
, we have that
\widetilde{N}_{m,a}\in\mathfrak{b}_{n_{a}}
.
Moreover we have that (as
(q_{m}^{*}\widehat{\Lambda})_{a}
is
\Gamma_{m}
-stable)

q_{m}^{*}\widehat{\Lambda}=\bigoplus_{a\in\mathcal{A}}(q_{m}^{*}\widehat{\Lambda})_{a}=\bigoplus_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\sigma_{\mu_{a}}(\pi_{\mu_{a}}(q_{m}^{*}\widehat{\Lambda})_{a}),

thus

q_{m}^{*}\widehat{\Lambda}\in Y_{\mu}^{\mathrm{rig}}\times_{\mathrm{Gr}^{\mathrm{rig}}_{n}}\prod_{a\in\mathcal{A}}GL_{n_{a}}/P_{\mu_{a}}=\prod_{a\in\mathcal{A}}Y_{\lambda_{a}}\times_{\mathrm{Gr}^{\mathrm{rig}}_{n_{a}}}GL_{n_{a}}/P_{\mu_{a}}

by Lemma
5.23
.
In particular, this implies that
(q_{m}^{*}\widehat{\Lambda})_{a}\in Y_{\lambda_{a}}
.
By Lemma
5.22
, we have

\pi_{\mu_{a}}((q_{m}^{*}\widehat{\Lambda})_{a})\in B_{n_{a}}w_{a}P_{\mu_{a}}/P_{\mu_{a}}

for
w_{a}\in W
such that
w_{a}w_{0}\mu_{a}=\lambda_{a}
.
This implies that the maps
(\widetilde{N}_{m,a},\pi_{\mu_{a}})_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}

factor through
\prod_{a\in\mathcal{A}}V_{w}^{\mu_{a}}
.
Then these maps give a map

(\widetilde{N}_{a},\pi_{\mu_{a}})_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}:\mathrm{Sp}(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{n},x_{n}})\rightarrow\prod_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}V_{w}^{\mu_{a}},

which is the desired left vertical arrow of the diagram (
6.6
).
By the definition of
N_{a}
and by Theorem
5.50
, we have in fact that

(\widetilde{N}_{a},\pi_{\mu_{a}})_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}\circ x_{n}=(N_{a},\pi_{\mu_{a}}(\Lambda_{a}))_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}
.
Observe that

\widehat{R}=\varprojlim_{m}\left(R/\mathfrak{n}^{m}\right),

where
\mathfrak{n}
is the maximal ideal corresponding to the point

(x_{n},(\pi_{\mu_{a}}(\Lambda_{a}))_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}})
.
Observe that by Lemma
5.23
and Lemma
5.22
, we have

\displaystyle X_{n}^{\prime}\times Y_{\mu}^{\mathrm{rig}}\times_{\mathrm{Gr}^{\mathrm{rig}}_{n}}\prod_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}(GL_{n_{a}}/P_{\mu_{a}})

\displaystyle=X_{n}^{\prime}\times\prod_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}Y_{\lambda_{a}}^{\mathrm{rig}}\times_{\mathrm{Gr}^{\mathrm{rig}}_{n_{a}}}(GL_{n_{a}}/P_{\mu_{a}})^{\mathrm{rig}}

\displaystyle=X_{n}^{\prime}\times\prod_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}(B_{n_{a}}w_{a}P_{\mu_{a}}/P_{\mu_{a}})^{\mathrm{rig}}.

Let us consider the maps

\displaystyle\mathrm{Sp}(R/(\mathfrak{n})^{m})\xrightarrow{p_{m}}\mathrm{Sp}(\widehat{R})\xrightarrow{q}

\displaystyle X_{n}^{\prime}\times\prod_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}(B_{n_{a}}w_{a}P_{\mu_{a}}/P_{\mu_{a}})^{\mathrm{rig}}\xrightarrow{p}X_{n}^{\prime}

and the pullback
p_{m}^{*}q^{*}p^{*}\mathbb{D}_{n}^{\prime}
.
The ring
R/(\mathfrak{n})^{m}

is a finite dimensional
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-algebra for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
, and by Remark
6.13

and Lemma
5.43
,
we have that
W_{\mathrm{dR}}^{+}(p_{m}^{*}q^{*}p^{*}\mathbb{D}_{n}^{\prime})
has the same mod
\mathbb{Z}
Sen weights as
W_{\mathrm{dR}}^{+}(x_{n}^{*}\mathbb{D}_{n})
.
After choosing a basis of
D_{\mathrm{pdR},a}(W_{\mathrm{dR}}(p_{m}^{*}q^{*}p^{*}\mathbb{D}_{n}^{\prime}[1/t])_{a})

respecting the triangulation of
p_{m}^{*}q^{*}p^{*}\mathbb{D}_{n}^{\prime}
for all
a\in{\mathcal{A}}
,
we have maps

\mathrm{Sp}(R/(\mathfrak{n})^{m})\rightarrow\prod_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}\mathfrak{b}_{n_{a}}^{\mathrm{rig}}\times(Bw_{a}P_{\mu_{a}}/P_{\mu_{a}})^{\mathrm{rig}}

given by the matrices
of the nilpotent endomorphisms
(N_{m,a})_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}
acting on

D_{\mathrm{pdR},a}(W_{\mathrm{dR}}(p_{m}^{*}q^{*}p^{*}\mathbb{D}_{n}^{\prime}[1/t])_{a})
,
which is then an element of
\prod_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}\mathfrak{b}_{n_{a}}^{\mathrm{rig}}
;
these maps give us the desired map

((\widetilde{N}_{a})_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}},\mathrm{id}):\mathrm{Sp}(\widehat{R})\rightarrow\prod_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}\mathfrak{b}_{n_{a}}^{\mathrm{rig}}\times(Bw_{a}P_{\mu_{a}}/P_{\mu_{a}})^{\mathrm{rig}}:

in fact we have

((\widetilde{N}_{a})_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}},\mathrm{id})\circ(x_{n},(\pi_{\mu_{a}}(\Lambda_{a}))_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}})=(N_{a},\pi_{\mu_{a}}(\Lambda_{a}))_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}
.
Now that we have defined the maps, it is still left to show that the diagram (
6.6
) commutes.
But this is very easy to see, in fact once we have chosen a basis of

\varprojlim_{m}D_{\mathrm{pdR},a}(W_{\mathrm{dR}}(q_{m}^{*}\hat{x}_{n}^{*}\mathbb{D}_{n}[1/t])_{a})

respecting the traingulation of
q_{m}^{*}\hat{x}_{n}^{*}\mathbb{D}_{n}
for all
a\in{\mathcal{A}}
,
we have that both compositions are given by

\left(\varprojlim_{m}\widetilde{N}_{m,a},\pi_{\mu_{a}}(\widehat{\Lambda}_{a})\right)_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}.

∎

Proposition 6.16
.

Let us assume the same conditions as Lemma
6.15
.
We have that

\mathrm{Spf}(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{n},x_{n}})\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Spf}(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{n}^{\prime},x_{n}^{\prime}})\times\mathrm{Spf}(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\prod_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}B_{n_{a}}w_{a}P_{\mu_{a}}/P_{\mu_{a}},(\Lambda,(\pi_{\mu_{a}}(\Lambda_{a}))_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}})})

is a closed immersion and
\mathrm{Spf}(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{n},x_{n}})
is cut out by

\sum_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}\dim B_{n_{a}}w_{a}P_{\mu_{a}}/P_{\mu_{a}}

equations.

Proof.

First of all we prove that the diagram
(
6.6
)
is a fiber product.
The functor of points of a
K
-formal scheme is determined by its values on
Artin local
K
-algebras with residue field
K
,
so let us assume that we have an Artin local
K
-algebra
A
with residue field
K

and maps

\displaystyle\mathrm{Spf}(A)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{f_{1}}\mathrm{Spf}(\widehat{R})

\displaystyle\mathrm{Spf}(A)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{f_{2}}\mathrm{Spf}(\widehat{S})

such that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Spf}(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Spf}(\widehat{R})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Spf}(\widehat{S})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Spf}(\widehat{T})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left((\widetilde{N}_{\bar{a}})_{\bar{a}\in\bar{\mathcal{A}}},\mathrm{id}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}

commutes.
By the fact that the formal scheme of the completion of the stalk prorepresents the functor
of lifts, we have that there exists a morphism

\mathrm{Sp}(A)\xrightarrow{f_{2}}\prod_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}V_{w_{a}}^{\mu_{a},\mathrm{rig}}

inducing
\mathrm{Sp}(A)\xrightarrow{f_{2}}\mathrm{Sp}(\widehat{S})

such that the diagram

(6.9)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Sp}(L)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Sp}(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Sp}(\widehat{R})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}V_{w_{a}}^{\mu_{a},\mathrm{rig}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}\mathfrak{b}_{n_{a}}^{\mathrm{rig}}\times(B_{n_{a}}w_{a}P_{\mu_{a}}/P_{\mu_{a}})^{\mathrm{rig}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(x_{n},(\pi_{\mu_{a}}(\Lambda_{a}))_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(N_{a},\pi_{\mu_{a}}(\Lambda_{a})\right)_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left((\widetilde{N}_{a})_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}},\mathrm{id}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}

commutes.
The map

X_{n}^{\prime}\times Y_{\mu}^{\mathrm{rig}}\times_{\mathrm{Gr}^{\mathrm{rig}}_{n}}\prod_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}(GL_{n_{a}}/P_{\mu_{a}})^{\mathrm{rig}}\xrightarrow{(\mathrm{id},\oplus_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}\sigma_{\mu_{a}})}X_{n}^{\prime}\times Y_{\mu}^{\mathrm{rig}}\times_{\mathrm{Gr}^{\mathrm{rig}}_{n}}\mathrm{Gr}^{\mathrm{rig}}_{n}

induces a map

\displaystyle u:\mathrm{Sp}(A)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{f_{1}}\mathrm{Sp}\left(\widehat{R}\right)\xrightarrow{(\mathrm{id},\oplus_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}\sigma_{\mu_{a}})}\mathrm{Sp}\left(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{n}^{\prime}\times Y_{\mu}^{\mathrm{rig}}\times_{\mathrm{Gr}^{\mathrm{rig}}_{n}}\mathrm{Gr}^{\mathrm{rig}}_{n},(x_{n},\Lambda)}\right),

in fact since
\Lambda_{a}
is
\Gamma_{m}
-stable,
we have that

\Lambda=\bigoplus_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}\sigma_{\mu_{a}}(\pi_{\mu_{a}}(\Lambda_{a}))

by Lemma
5.49
.
We show that

\mathrm{im}(u)\subseteq\mathrm{Sp}\left(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{n},x_{n}}\right):

since
X_{n}
is the closed locus
of
X_{n}^{\prime}\times Y_{\mu}^{\mathrm{rig}}
of
\Gamma_{m}
-stable lattices, we just need to show that

u^{*}\mathbb{L}
is
\Gamma_{m}
-stable, where
\mathbb{L}
is the universal lattice above

Y_{\mu}^{\mathrm{rig}}
.
Observe that
u^{*}\mathbb{L}=\bigoplus_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}u^{*}\mathbb{L}_{a}

for some smaller
A\llbracket t\rrbracket
-modules
u^{*}\mathbb{L}_{a}
,
as
u
factors through
\mathrm{Sp}(\widehat{R})
by definition:
in fact we have that by definition of
R
,
(\mathrm{id},\oplus_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}\sigma_{\mu_{a}})^{*}\mathbb{L}

decomposes into a direct sum of sub-
\widehat{R}\llbracket t\rrbracket
-modules.
Thanks to Theorem
5.50
, showing that
u^{*}\mathbb{L}
is
\Gamma_{m}
-stable is equivalent
to showing that for all
a\in{\mathcal{A}}
we have that

\pi_{\mu_{a}}(u^{*}\mathbb{L}_{a})
is a stable filtration under the nilpotent
endomorphism
N_{a}
given by the
\mathbb{G}_{a}
-representation associated to the
A
-module

D_{\mathrm{pdR},a}(W_{\mathrm{dR}}(u^{*}\mathbb{D}_{n}^{\prime}[1/t])_{a})
.
In other words, we want to show that the image of the map

\displaystyle\mathrm{Sp}(A)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{u}\mathrm{Sp}\left(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{n}^{\prime}\times Y_{\mu}^{\mathrm{rig}}\times_{\mathrm{Gr}^{\mathrm{rig}}_{n}}\mathrm{Gr}^{\mathrm{rig}}_{n},(x_{n},\Lambda)}\right)\xrightarrow{(\mathrm{id},(\pi_{\mu_{a}})_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}})}\mathrm{Sp}\left(\widehat{R}\right)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{((\widetilde{N_{a}})_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}},\mathrm{id})}\prod_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}\mathfrak{b}_{n_{a}}^{\mathrm{rig}}\times(B_{n_{a}}w_{a}P_{\mu_{a}}/P_{\mu_{a}})^{\mathrm{rig}}

is contained in
\prod_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}V_{w_{a}}^{\mu_{a},\mathrm{rig}}
.
Now notice that

\displaystyle((\widetilde{N_{a}})_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}},\mathrm{id})\circ(\mathrm{id},(\pi_{\mu_{a}})_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}})\circ u

\displaystyle=((\widetilde{N_{a}})_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}},\mathrm{id})\circ(\mathrm{id},(\pi_{\mu_{a}})_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}})\circ(\mathrm{id},\oplus_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}\sigma_{\mu_{a}})\circ f_{1}

\displaystyle=((\widetilde{N}_{a})_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}},\mathrm{id})\circ f_{1}=i\circ f_{2},

where the second to last equality is due to the fact that

(\pi_{\mu_{a}})_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}\circ(\oplus_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}\sigma_{\mu_{a}})=\mathrm{id}

and the last equality is due to the commutativity of the diagram (
6.9
).
Hence we have proved that the map
u

factors through
\mathrm{Sp}(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{n},x_{n}})
.
Now notice that the diagram

(6.10)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Sp}(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Sp}\left(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{n},x_{n}}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Sp}(\widehat{R})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}V_{w_{a}}^{\mu_{a},\mathrm{rig}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}\mathfrak{b}_{n_{a}}^{\mathrm{rig}}\times(B_{n_{a}}w_{a}P_{\mu_{a}}/P_{\mu_{a}})^{\mathrm{rig}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\mathrm{id},(\pi_{\mu_{a}})_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\widetilde{N}_{a},\pi_{\mu_{a}}\right)_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left((\widetilde{N}_{a})_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}},\mathrm{id}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}

is commutative:

(\mathrm{id},(\pi_{\mu_{a}})_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}})\circ u=(\mathrm{id},(\pi_{\mu_{a}})_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}})\circ(\mathrm{id},\oplus_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}\sigma_{\mu_{a}})\circ f_{1}=f_{1}

and

\displaystyle i\circ(\widetilde{N}_{a},\pi_{\mu_{a}})_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}\circ u

\displaystyle=((\widetilde{N}_{a})_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}},\mathrm{id})\circ(\mathrm{id},(\pi_{\mu_{a}})_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}})\circ(\mathrm{id},\oplus_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}\sigma_{\mu_{a}})\circ f_{1}

\displaystyle=((\widetilde{N}_{a})_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}},\mathrm{id})\circ f_{1}=i\circ f_{2}.

Since
i
is a closed immersion, we can conclude that

(\widetilde{N}_{a},\pi_{\mu_{a}})_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}\circ u=f_{2}
.
Finally, assume there exists
v:\mathrm{Sp}(A)\rightarrow\mathrm{Sp}(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{n},x_{n}})
such that
the diagram (
6.10
) is commutative when we substitute
v
to
u
.
We will denote by
(u^{\prime},\Lambda_{u}),(v^{\prime},\Lambda_{v})\in X_{n}(A)
the maps
corresponding to
u
and
v
respectively.
Since
\Lambda_{u}
is
\Gamma_{m}
-stable and we have
\Lambda_{u}\otimes_{A}L=\Lambda
,
we have a decomposition

\Lambda_{u}=\bigoplus_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}\Lambda_{u,a}
and
similarly for
\Lambda_{v}
.
Then we have

\displaystyle\left(u^{\prime},\Lambda_{u},(\pi_{\mu_{a}}(\Lambda_{u,a}))_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}\right)

\displaystyle=(\mathrm{id},(\pi_{\mu_{a}})_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}})\circ u=f_{1}=(\mathrm{id},(\pi_{\mu_{a}})_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}})\circ v

\displaystyle=\left(v^{\prime},\Lambda_{v},(\pi_{\mu_{a}}(\Lambda_{v,a}))_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}\right),

which implies that
u=(u^{\prime},\Lambda_{u})=(v^{\prime},\Lambda_{v})=v
.
In particular we have that the diagram

(6.11)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Sp}\left(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{n},x_{n}}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Sp}(\widehat{R})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}V_{w_{a}}^{\mu_{a},\mathrm{rig}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}\mathfrak{b}_{n_{a}}^{\mathrm{rig}}\times(Bw_{a}P_{\mu_{a}}/P_{\mu_{a}})^{\mathrm{rig}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\mathrm{id},(\pi_{\mu_{a}})_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\widetilde{N}_{a},\pi_{\mu_{a}}\right)_{a\in\mathcal{A}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left((\widetilde{N}_{a})_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}},\mathrm{id}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}

is a fiber product and hence
the morphism

(\mathrm{id},(\pi_{\mu_{a}})_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}):\mathrm{Sp}(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{n},x_{n}})\rightarrow\mathrm{Sp}(\widehat{R})

is a closed immersion.
Finally, notice that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Sp}(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Sp}\left(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{n},x_{n}}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Sp}(L)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{n}}

commutes: observe that

(\mathrm{id},(\pi_{\mu_{a}})_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}})\circ u\circ a=f_{1}\circ a=(x_{n},\pi_{\mu_{a}}(\Lambda_{a})_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}})=(\mathrm{id},(\pi_{\mu_{a}})_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}})\circ x_{n},

where the first equality is given by the commutativity of diagram (
6.10
),
the second equality is by commutativity of diagram (
6.9
) and the last equality
is by definition of the map
(\mathrm{id},(\pi_{\mu_{a}})_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}})
as in (
6.8
).
By the fact that
(\mathrm{id},(\pi_{\mu_{a}})_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}})
is a closed immersion and hence it has a left inverse,
we obtain that
u\circ a=x_{n}
.
As a consequence, we have that there exists a unique map

u:\mathrm{Spf}(A)\rightarrow\mathrm{Spf}(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{n},x_{n}})

such that the following diagram is commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Spf}(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Spf}(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{n},x_{n}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Spf}(\widehat{R})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Spf}(\widehat{S})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Spf}(\widehat{T}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}

This means that the diagram (
6.6
) is a fiber product.
Notice that, since
B_{n_{a}}w_{a}P_{\mu_{a}}/P_{\mu_{a}}
is an affine space, we have

\mathrm{Sp}(L\langle T_{1},\dots,T_{M}\rangle)=\prod_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}(B_{n_{a}}w_{a}P_{\mu_{a}}/P_{\mu_{a}})^{\mathrm{rig}},

where
M\coloneqq\sum_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}\dim B_{n_{a}}w_{a}P_{\mu_{a}}/P_{\mu_{a}}
.
Recall that
X_{n}^{\prime}=\mathrm{Sp}(L\langle x_{1},\dots,x_{d}\rangle)
, since it is a
vector bundle of dimension

d\coloneqq\sum_{i=2}^{n}\dim_{L}H^{1}_{\phi,\Gamma}(D_{i-1}^{\prime}(\delta_{i}^{-1}x^{\mu_{i}}))

over an affinoid neighbourhood
\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}

of
(\delta_{1}x^{-\mu_{1}},\dots,\delta_{n}x^{-\mu_{n}})
in
\mathcal{T}^{n}
(
d=1
in case
n=2
).
Thus

\displaystyle\widehat{R}

\displaystyle=L\langle x_{1},\dots,x_{d},T_{1},\dots,T_{M}\rangle^{^}_{(x_{n},(\pi_{\mu_{a}}(\Lambda_{a}))_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}})}

\displaystyle=L\llbracket x_{1},\dots,x_{d},T_{1},\dots,T_{M}\rrbracket

\displaystyle=L\llbracket x_{1},\dots,x_{d}\rrbracket\otimes_{L}L\llbracket T_{1},\dots,T_{M}\rrbracket

\displaystyle=L\langle x_{1},\dots,x_{d}\rangle^{^}_{x_{n}^{\prime}}\widehat{\otimes}_{L}L\langle T_{1},\dots,T_{M}\rangle^{^}_{(\pi_{\mu_{a}}(\Lambda_{a}))_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}}

\displaystyle=\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{n}^{\prime},x_{n}^{\prime}}\widehat{\otimes}_{L}\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\prod_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}Bw_{a}P_{\mu_{a}}/P_{\mu_{a}},(\pi_{\mu_{a}}(\Lambda_{a}))_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}},

where the completions are with respect to the subscript maximal ideals.
Thus we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Spf}(\widehat{R})=\mathrm{Spf}(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{n}^{\prime},x_{n}^{\prime}})\times\mathrm{Spf}(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\prod_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}B_{n_{a}}w_{a}P_{\mu_{a}}/P_{\mu_{a}},(\pi_{\mu_{a}}(\Lambda_{a}))_{\bar{a}\in\bar{\mathcal{A}}}}).

Recall by Remark
5.27
that

V_{w_{a}}^{\mu_{a}}\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{b}_{n_{a}}\times B_{n_{a}}w_{a}P_{\mu_{a}}/P_{\mu_{a}}
is cut out by
exactly
\dim B_{n_{a}}w_{a}P_{\mu_{a}}/P_{\mu_{a}}
equations.
Finally by the fact that the square (
6.6
) is a fiber product,
it is then obvious that

\mathrm{Spf}(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{n},x_{n}})\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Spf}(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{n}^{\prime},x_{n}^{\prime}})\times\mathrm{Spf}(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\prod_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}B_{n_{a}}w_{a}P_{\mu_{a}}/P_{\mu_{a}},(\pi_{\mu_{a}}(\Lambda_{a}))_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}})

is a closed immersion and
\mathrm{Spf}(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{n},x_{n}})
is cut out by

\sum_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}\dim B_{n_{a}}w_{a}P_{\mu_{a}}/P_{\mu_{a}}
equations.
∎

We will show that the dimension of
\mathrm{Spec}(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{n},x_{n}})
is
at least the dimension of
\mathrm{Spec}(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{n}^{\prime},x_{n}^{\prime}})
.

Corollary 6.17
.

We have

\dim\mathrm{Spec}(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{n},x_{n}})\geqslant\dim\mathrm{Spec}(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{n}^{\prime},x_{n}^{\prime}}).

Proof.

Let
M\coloneqq\sum_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}\dim B_{n_{a}}w_{a}P_{\mu_{a}}/P_{\mu_{a}}
.
By Proposition
6.16
, we have that there are

f_{1},\dots,f_{M}\in\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{n}^{\prime},x_{n}^{\prime}}\llbracket T_{1},\dots T_{M}\rrbracket
such that

\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{n},x_{n}}=\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{n}^{\prime},x_{n}^{\prime}}\llbracket T_{1},\dots,T_{M}\rrbracket/(f_{1},\dots,f_{M}).

This immediately concludes the proof.
∎

We make use of the following result from
[
15
, Exercise 3.20]
.

Theorem 6.18
.

Let
X
be an integral scheme of finite type over a field
k
.
For any closed point
P\in X
,
\dim X=\dim\mathcal{O}_{X,P}
.

Remark 6.19
.

Notice first of all that since the dimension of a scheme
X
doesn’t depend on the reducible structure
of
\mathcal{O}_{X}
, we have the above result for irreducible schemes more in general.
Moreover observe that the notion of "dimension" for a scheme (resp. a rigid space) is
equal to the dimension of the ring associated to an open affine (resp. affinoid) subspace
of the scheme (resp. rigid space).
As a consequence, Theorem
6.18
is valid also for irreducible rigid spaces.

Now let us go back to considering only the 2-dimensional case.
We show that any irreducible component of
X
has dimension at least the dimension
of the regular locus.

Theorem 6.20
.

Let
Z
be an irreducible component of
X_{n}
containing the point
x_{n}
.
Then

\dim Z\geqslant\dim(F^{-1}(\mathcal{W}_{n}^{\mathrm{reg}})).

Proof.

Since
Z
is irreducible, we get

\dim Z=\dim\mathrm{Spec}(\mathcal{O}_{Z,z})

for any point
z\in Z(K)
by Remark
6.19
.
For a Noetherian local ring
A
, we have
\dim\mathrm{Spec}(A)=\dim\mathrm{Spec}(\widehat{A})
,
thus

\dim\mathrm{Spec}(\mathcal{O}_{X_{n},x_{n}})=\dim\mathrm{Spec}(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{n},x_{n}}).

Furthermore we have that

\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{n},x_{n}}=\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{n}^{\prime},x_{n}^{\prime}}\llbracket T_{1},\dots,T_{M}\rrbracket/(f_{1},\dots,f_{M})

by Proposition
6.16
.
Therefore

\dim\mathrm{Spec}(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{Z,x_{n}})\geqslant\dim\mathrm{Spec}(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{X^{\prime},x_{n}^{\prime}}).

Finally putting the above together, we get

\displaystyle\dim Z

\displaystyle=\dim\mathrm{Spec}(\mathcal{O}_{Z,x_{n}})=\dim\mathrm{Spec}(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{Z,x_{n}})\geqslant\dim\mathrm{Spec}(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{n}^{\prime},x_{n}^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=\dim\mathrm{Spec}(\mathcal{O}_{X_{n}^{\prime},x_{n}^{\prime}})=\dim(F^{-1}(\mathcal{W}_{n}^{\mathrm{reg}})),

where the last equality is given by Remark
6.19
and Remark
7.18

(Remark
6.11
for dimension
n=2
).
∎

We are now back to considering the case of dimension 2.

Lemma 6.21
.

Let
W
be the intersection of
\mathcal{W}_{2}^{\mathrm{reg}}
and
\mathrm{im}(F)
in
\mathcal{W}^{2}
, let

W^{\prime}\coloneqq\{(\eta_{1},\eta_{2}\cdot x^{n+1})\colon(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})\in W\}\subset H(\mathcal{U}^{\prime})

and let
T^{\prime}
be the intersection of
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
and
H^{-1}(W^{\prime})
in
\mathcal{T}^{2}
.
Recall the definition of
X^{\prime}\coloneqq\mathcal{U}^{\prime}\times\mathbb{A}_{L}^{1,\mathrm{an}}
for
some open neighbourhood
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
of
(\delta_{1},\delta_{2}x^{\mu_{2}})
in
\mathcal{T}^{2}

as in the proof of Proposition
4.2
.
The map

(6.12)

\pi:F^{-1}(W)\rightarrow T^{\prime}\times\mathbb{A}_{L}^{1,\mathrm{an}}\subset\mathcal{U}^{\prime}\times\mathbb{A}_{L}^{1,\mathrm{an}}=X^{\prime}

given by the restriction of the map
X\rightarrow X^{\prime}
to
F^{-1}(W)
is a homeomorphism.

Proof.

Let
z=(z^{\prime},\Lambda_{z})\in F^{-1}(W)
with

z^{\prime}=(\widetilde{\eta}_{1}^{\prime},\widetilde{\eta}_{2}^{\prime},a)\in\mathcal{U}^{\prime}\times\mathbb{A}_{L}^{1,\mathrm{an}}=X^{\prime}
.
Then by Theorem
6.8
, we have
that
z^{*}\mathbb{D}
has parameters
(\widetilde{\eta}_{1}^{\prime},\widetilde{\eta}_{2}^{\prime}\cdot x^{-n-1})
.
On the other hand
z\in F^{-1}(W)
, hence
H(\widetilde{\eta}_{1}^{\prime},\widetilde{\eta}_{2}^{\prime}\cdot x^{-n-1})\in W
,
which proves that
H(\widetilde{\eta}_{1}^{\prime},\widetilde{\eta}_{2}^{\prime})\in W^{\prime}
and as a consequence

(\widetilde{\eta}_{1}^{\prime},\widetilde{\eta}_{2}^{\prime})\in T^{\prime}
.
This shows that the above map is well defined and has image in
T^{\prime}\times\mathbb{A}_{L}^{1,\mathrm{an}}
.
We will now show that the map
\pi
is bijective.
Notice that we have the following commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{-1}(W)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\coloneqq H^{-1}(W)\cap\mathcal{U}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{\prime}\times\mathbb{A}_{L}^{1,\mathrm{an}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{\prime},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

\scriptstyle\sim

where the map
G:F^{-1}(W)\rightarrow T
is the restriction
of the map
G
in (
7.2
) to
F^{-1}(W)
and the right vertical map is just
the twisting of characters.
We will then show that

\pi:F^{-1}(W)=\bigcup_{(\widetilde{\eta}_{1},\widetilde{\eta}_{2})\in T}G^{-1}(\widetilde{\eta}_{1},\widetilde{\eta}_{2})\rightarrow\bigcup_{(\widetilde{\eta}_{1},\widetilde{\eta}_{2}x^{n+1})\in T^{\prime}}(\widetilde{\eta}_{1},\widetilde{\eta}_{2}x^{n+1})\times\mathbb{A}_{L}^{1,\mathrm{an}}=T^{\prime}\times\mathbb{A}_{L}^{1,\mathrm{an}}

restricts to a bijection on the fibers

G^{-1}(\widetilde{\eta}_{1},\widetilde{\eta}_{2})\xrightarrow{\pi}(\widetilde{\eta}_{1},\widetilde{\eta}_{2}x^{n+1})\times\mathbb{A}_{L}^{1,\mathrm{an}}

for all
(\widetilde{\eta}_{1},\widetilde{\eta}_{2})\in T(K)
for some
K
extension of
L
.
It is enough to notice that we have a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{-1}(\widetilde{\eta}_{1},\widetilde{\eta}_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\widetilde{\eta}_{1},\widetilde{\eta}_{2}x^{n+1})\times\mathbb{A}_{L}^{1,\mathrm{an}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}_{\phi,\Gamma}(\mathcal{R}_{K}(\widetilde{\eta}_{1}/\widetilde{\eta}_{2}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}_{\phi,\Gamma}(\mathcal{R}_{K}(\widetilde{\eta}_{1}/\widetilde{\eta}_{2}\cdot x^{-n-1})),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\ref{fiber of G})}

\scriptstyle\sim

\scriptstyle\sim

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

where the map (
6.4
) is a bijection as proved in Lemma
6.9

and the notation
H^{1}_{\phi,\Gamma}(\mathcal{R}_{K}(\widetilde{\eta}_{1}/\widetilde{\eta}_{2}))
(resp.

H^{1}_{\phi,\Gamma}(\mathcal{R}_{K}(\widetilde{\eta}_{1}/\widetilde{\eta}_{2}\cdot x^{-n-1}))
) indicates
the rigid space associated to the
k(\widetilde{\underline{\eta}})
-vector space

H^{1}_{\phi,\Gamma}(\mathcal{R}_{K}(\widetilde{\eta}_{1}/\widetilde{\eta}_{2}))

(resp.
H^{1}_{\phi,\Gamma}(\mathcal{R}_{K}(\widetilde{\eta}_{1}/\widetilde{\eta}_{2}\cdot x^{-n-1}))
);
the horizontal bijection is given by Lemma
5.16

(in fact
\mathrm{wt}(\widetilde{\eta}_{1}/\widetilde{\eta}_{2})\notin\mathbb{Z}
, otherwise
H(\widetilde{\eta_{1}},\widetilde{\eta_{2}})\notin\mathcal{W}_{2}^{\mathrm{reg}}
);
finally the rightmost vertical bijection is given by Remark
6.5
.
We have thus proved that
\pi
is a bijection.
We moreover have that

\displaystyle F^{-1}(W)

\displaystyle=G^{-1}(H^{-1}(W)\cap\mathcal{U})=G^{-1}(T)

\displaystyle=X\times_{\mathcal{U}}T=X^{\prime}\times\mathrm{Gr}^{\mathrm{rig}}_{2}\times_{\mathrm{Gr}^{\mathrm{rig}}_{2}\times\mathrm{Gr}^{\mathrm{rig}}_{2}}Y_{(0,-n-1)}\times_{\mathcal{U}}T

\displaystyle=\mathcal{U}^{\prime}\times\mathbb{A}^{1,\mathrm{an}}_{L}\times\mathrm{Gr}^{\mathrm{rig}}_{2}\times_{\mathrm{Gr}^{\mathrm{rig}}_{2}\times\mathrm{Gr}^{\mathrm{rig}}_{2}}Y_{(0,-n-1)}\times_{\mathcal{U}}T

\displaystyle=\mathcal{U}^{\prime}\times_{\mathcal{U}}T\times\mathbb{A}^{1,\mathrm{an}}_{L}\times\mathrm{Gr}^{\mathrm{rig}}_{2}\times_{\mathrm{Gr}^{\mathrm{rig}}_{2}\times\mathrm{Gr}^{\mathrm{rig}}_{2}}Y_{(0,-n-1)}

\displaystyle=T^{\prime}\times\mathbb{A}^{1,\mathrm{an}}_{L}\times\mathrm{Gr}^{\mathrm{rig}}_{2}\times_{\mathrm{Gr}^{\mathrm{rig}}_{2}\times\mathrm{Gr}^{\mathrm{rig}}_{2}}Y_{(0,-n-1)}.

Recall that for locally ringed spaces
X
,
Y
over a field
k
, the natural projection
X\times_{k}Y\rightarrow X

is open.
In our case, this translates to the map

\pi:T^{\prime}\times\mathbb{A}_{L}^{1,\mathrm{an}}\times\mathrm{Gr}^{\mathrm{rig}}_{2}\times_{\mathrm{Gr}^{\mathrm{rig}}_{2}\times\mathrm{Gr}^{\mathrm{rig}}_{2}}Y_{(0,-n-1)}\rightarrow T^{\prime}\times\mathbb{A}_{L}^{1,\mathrm{an}}

being open.
We have than shown that
\pi
is continuous, open and a bijection, hence a homeomorphism.
∎

In the following we will make use of this result from
[
15
, Exercise 3.22]
.

Theorem 6.22
.

Let
f:X\rightarrow Y
be a dominant morphism of integral schemes of finite type over a field
k
.
Let
e=\dim X-\dim Y
be the relative dimension of
X
over
Y
.
For any point
y\in f(X)
we have that every irreducible component of the fiber

X_{y}
has dimension
\geqslant e
.

Remark 6.23
.

For the same reasons as written in Remark
6.19
, we have that
Theorem
6.22
holds for irreducible rigid spaces.

Theorem 6.24
.

Let
Z
be an irreducible component of
X
containing the point
x
.
Then the space
Z
is equal to the closure of
F^{-1}(\mathcal{W}_{2}^{\mathrm{reg}})
;
in particular, there is only one irreducible component containing the point
x
.

Proof.

Let
W\coloneqq\mathcal{W}_{2}^{\mathrm{reg}}\cap\mathrm{im}(F)\subset\mathcal{W}^{2}

and let
Z^{\prime}
be the closure of
F^{-1}(W)
in
X
.
By Lemma
6.21
, we have that
F^{-1}(W)
is irreducible,
as
X^{\prime}
is obviously irreducible; as a consequence
Z^{\prime}
is irreducible, since
closure of irreducible.
Now we show that
Z^{\prime}
is an irreducible component of
X
: assume
\widetilde{Z}\supset Z^{\prime}

is an irreducible component of
X
containing
Z^{\prime}
.
Then
F^{-1}(W)
is open in
Z^{\prime}
and
\widetilde{Z}
, so

\dim\widetilde{Z}=\dim F^{-1}(W)=\dim Z^{\prime}.

This shows that
Z^{\prime}=\widetilde{Z}
, since they are both irreducible of the same dimension.
Now we prove that
Z^{\prime}
is equal to
Z
:
assume by contradiction that
Z\neq Z^{\prime}
and let

V\coloneqq X\setminus\bigcup_{Z^{\prime\prime}\neq Z}Z^{\prime\prime}\subset Z

where the union ranges over all the irreducible components
Z^{\prime\prime}
of
X
that are not
Z
.
We have that
V
is open and irreducible and so

F(V)
is irreducible.
Moreover we have that
